## Egypt — English Primary 4 Term 1 (read 2026-10-25)

Source: the newest copy on the ministry e-learning host
(2026_2027 path, last-modified 2026-08-17); an older 2026-path copy
exists and was NOT taken — latest edition wins. Working file
`English_language_prim4_t1.pdf`, 29,131,912 bytes, ends %%EOF, PDF
title "ENG G4 T1", 121 pages, IMAGE-ONLY (zero text on every page —
read visually). Printed folio = PDF page throughout.

Identity: cover "English / Primary 4 / Term 1" with a NEW design —
cartoon cover art, but the inside plates are mostly REAL
PHOTOGRAPHS. © 2025 Ministry of Education and Technical Education
(p.2, with the ministry dedication). Back page (p.120): ISBN
978-633-8475-11-6, deposit no. 2026/9307, Arabic print spec says
124 pages (the PDF holds 121 — same print-vs-PDF gap as P3 T1),
book code 10/4/1/1/40/58, printing 2026–2027; developed/revised/
supervised by Prof. Zeinab El-Naggar, Prof. Attia El-Sayed, Prof.
Nagwa Younis, Dr. Mai Kamal, Ms. Amira Awaad and others; Selah El
Telmeez; British Council + CACD marks. Ministry message (p.6)
names the "Primary 4 Framework for Egyptian Learners" and promises
each unit a fable inspired by Middle Eastern folklore.

Shape: Scope and Sequence now spans pp.3–5 with a Life Skill(s) /
Core Value(s) / Integrated learning Activities column set. Six
units, each with a Learning Outcomes opener, five lessons in a
fixed rhythm — L1 words + dialog, L2 text + Language in Use box,
L3 Story Time + Pronunciation, L4 Writing, L5 Think and Create —
then Quick Review + a "Now I can…" thumbs Self-Assessment. Review
1 after U3 (pp.57–60), Review 2 after U6 (pp.109–113). Glossary
pp.114–116 (~110 words BY CATEGORY with one-line definitions),
blank Notes pp.117–119, back-cover values page (p.121).

Unit spans: U1 The Five Senses pp.7–22 · U2 My Community
pp.23–38 · U3 Animals In Our World pp.39–56 · U4 Egypt — My
Homeland pp.61–78 · U5 A Day At Work pp.79–94 · U6 The Hundred
Dresses pp.95–108 (a STORY unit — a real published story, adapted;
no Language in Use).

GRAMMAR SPINE (Language in Use boxes, book order):
1. **Present Simple Affirmative** (U1, p.13) — used for daily
   habits and routines; I/you/we/they and plural nouns take the
   plain verb; he/she/it and singular nouns add -s/-es/-ies (the
   book shows plays / brushes / carries). A compressed restatement
   of P3 T1's full spelling rules.
2. **Present Simple Negative and Question** (U2, p.29) — the
   book's full form rules: don't with I/you/we/they and plurals,
   doesn't with he/she/it and singulars; questions open with do /
   does and the plain verb.
3. **Comparative adjectives** (U3, p.45) — used to compare two
   people, things or animals, with all four spelling rules stated:
   add -er; double the last letter after a short vowel (big →
   bigger); words ending -e add -r; consonant + -y changes to -ier.
   Every example runs with "than".
4. **Prepositions of Place** (U4, p.67) — "show where things are,"
   taught on a cat-and-box plate: in, on, in front of, behind,
   next to, between. (Union with P3 T1's set — which had under —
   is now the fullest place set in the Egypt run.)
5. **Superlative adjectives** (U5, p.85) — "the greatest degree
   of something": the same four spelling rules with -est and
   "the" in front (tallest, biggest, nicest, happiest).

Also named in print: **prefixes** (U2 Tip, p.33) — a prefix is a
small group of letters added to the beginning of a word to change
its meaning; dis-, il- and ir- all shown making opposites
(dislike, illegal, irregular, dishonest, irresponsible…), the
first prefix teaching in the Egypt run. **Big numbers** (U4 L3,
p.68): reading numbers up to a billion, built up place by place —
a billion is stated as a thousand million.

PHONICS SPINE: short vs long vowels defined in plain words (U1,
p.17 — short sounds are quick and not the letter's name; long
sounds say the letter's name) with an a/o/e table in the U1
self-assessment. Voiced/voiceless pairs, the series' running
thread: /f/ voiceless vs /v/ voiced (U2, p.32) and /p/ voiceless
vs /b/ voiced, with how to make them — lips together, blow out
air for p, use your voice for b (U4, p.69). Blends fr / dr / pl
(U3, p.49) with the blend definition restated in the book's own
short form (joining two or more sounds to read a word — frown,
fruit, drum, draw, plane, plant). Silent e restated (U5, p.89):
a silent e after one consonant makes the vowel before it long
(rope, slide, cube, face, stone, flute, gate, nine).

UNIT NOTES:
- U1 The Five Senses — the five senses taught through REAL
  PHOTOS (a child tasting ice cream, an eye, an ear, a finger on
  a cactus, smelling a flower); "My Healthy Day" routine text;
  Story Time is the Goha folk tale of the soup's smell paid for
  with the sound of coins (Middle Eastern folklore, as the
  ministry message promised); the story-elements Tip (p.15) is
  the fullest printed form yet — Characters, Setting, and Plot
  broken into Beginning / Problem / Middle / Ending; Writing =
  sequence words extended to First, Next, Then, After that,
  Finally; L5 is a healthy-eating presentation with peer star
  feedback.
- U2 My Community — helping words by real photos (collect,
  plant, clean, help, feed, volunteer); a problems-and-solutions
  dialog (traffic, trash); L3 is Egypt's own culture text: the
  Delta in the north vs Upper Egypt in the south — Spring
  Festival, the sugar cane harvest, Nubia's painted houses,
  galabeyas and turbans; Writing = a diary entry, with the book's
  four printed parts (date, greeting, details, closing) and a
  four-paragraph frame; L5 = a solutions poster.
- U3 Animals In Our World — six animals by real photos with
  one-line descriptions; a taking-care-of-animals text saturated
  with comparatives in use before the rule box; Story Time is
  the Aesop fable of the old lion and the fox, with the moral
  defined again in the book's simplest words (the lesson a story
  teaches us, showing right from wrong); Writing = a research
  report on endangered animals (the Arabian Leopard and the Red
  Sea Dugong, real photos) with the book's six printed report
  parts — title, introduction, facts, examples, solutions,
  conclusion — and a writing frame to match.
- U4 Egypt — My Homeland — famous places by real photos
  (Pyramids, Nile, Cairo Tower, Siwa Oasis, Egyptian Museum,
  Khan Al-Khalili) with a tour-guide dialog; city places text
  carrying prepositions in use before the rule box; the big-
  numbers lesson; Writing = a blog post, with the book's six
  printed blog parts (clear title, writer's name, date,
  pictures, interesting details, descriptive words) and a
  sense-words Tip tying descriptive writing back to U1; L5
  dissects a travel leaflet and names its parts (main heading,
  sub-heading, image, detailed description, call to action —
  each defined in a line).
- U5 A Day At Work — jobs by real photos keyed to uniforms
  (doctor, nurse, teacher, engineer, firefighter, police
  officer) with a what-do-you-do / what-do-you-wear dialog;
  "Four Amazing Jobs" (a mentor, a wildlife photographer, a
  puppeteer, a voice-over actor) seeding superlatives in the
  text; Story Time is the folk tale of the carpenter's last
  house (work ethics — the S&S value is integrity); Writing =
  non-fiction, with the book's truth test ("Is the information
  true? Did I make anything up?") and a paragraph's parts named
  — introductory sentence, true details, adjectives, closing
  sentence; L5 = origami, defined in print as the Japanese art
  of paper folding.
- U6 The Hundred Dresses — a whole unit on ONE real published
  story, credited on the outcomes page to its author, Eleanor
  Estes, in a simplified retelling (recorded here as summary
  only): a quiet girl who wears the same faded dress daily yet
  speaks of a hundred dresses at home is teased by two
  classmates; her hundred dress DRAWINGS win the school
  competition after her family has moved away; the two girls
  write to apologize, she answers kindly and gifts them the
  drawings, and they resolve to speak up against bullying.
  Front matter: picture vocabulary + a definitions page in the
  book's plain style (bullying, apologize, behavior…) + the four
  characters named. Follow-up work: who-said-it, event ordering,
  a story-elements diagram (Setting / Characters / Moral, the
  moral stated in one line), agree-or-disagree judgment work
  with reasons, writing back in the main character's voice, and
  a word-search self-assessment. The Story title's biggest
  single-book unit yet — a real literature text, not a made-up
  passage.

Back cover (p.121): five values messages with real photos of
children — helping others, respecting differences, kind words,
eating fruit and vegetables, daily exercise.

Filed from this read: blends sources (fr/dr/pl + the restated
blend definition, p.49); word-families sources (short/long vowel
definition, p.17; silent e restated, p.89); two-letter-sounds
sources (the f/v and p/b voicing pairs, pp.32, 69). The rest —
present simple in all three forms, comparatives, superlatives,
the fullest preposition set, prefixes, big numbers, the writing
frames (diary, report, blog, leaflet, non-fiction paragraph),
the story-elements plot arc, and one whole adapted literature
unit — is recorded here and slotted in CURRICULUM.md.

**PRIMARY 4 TERM 1 FULLY READ — 2026-10-25.** Egypt progress: 9
of 26 English books read (KG1, KG2, P1 T1+T2, P2 T1+T2, P3 T1+T2,
P4 T1). Next: Primary 4 Term 2.

## Egypt — English Primary 4 Term 2 (read 2026-10-25)

Source: the ministry e-learning host's 2026 path — the ONLY copy
the host carries for this term (no 2026_2027 copy exists; probed
and 404). Working file `English_Prim4_Tr2.pdf`, 220,739,768 bytes
(the series' biggest file by far — uncompressed pages), ends
%%EOF, PDF title "Primary SB4 final Darwish COL.", 112 pages,
essentially image-only (234 stray text chars — read visually).
Printed folio = PDF page throughout.

Identity: cover "English / Primary 4 / Term 2 / 2025/2026" (p.1),
same new design as Term 1. © 2025 MoE dedication (p.2). Back page
(p.112): "English Primary 4 – Term 2, Academic Year 2025/2026";
developed and produced with the Content Department Team at Selah
El Telmeez; reviewed by Prof. Zeinab El-Naggar, Prof. Attia
El-Sayed, Prof. Nagwa Younis, Dr. Mai Kamal, Ms. Amira Awaad;
CACD team; general supervision Dr. Akram Hassan Mohammed;
British Council mark; printing house Darwish (matching the PDF
title); Arabic print spec says 116 pages (the PDF holds 112).
NO ISBN and NO deposit number are printed on this back page —
Term 1 had both.

Shape: Scope and Sequence pp.3–8 (one page per unit this time).
Six units in the same five-lesson rhythm as Term 1, Quick Review
+ star-based Self-Assessment table ("I got it / I'm not sure /
I need help") per unit; Review 1 after U3 (pp.59–60), Review 2
after U6 (pp.105–107); Glossary pp.108–111, ALPHABETICAL this
time (~110 words, one-line definitions). Unit spans: U1 This is
Where I Live pp.10–26 · U2 Our World, Our Responsibility
pp.27–41 · U3 What's in the Package? pp.42–58 · U4 Let's
Celebrate pp.61–76 · U5 Exploring Wonders in Egypt pp.77–92 ·
U6 The Lost Kite pp.93–104 (a STORY unit — one whole original
story).

GRAMMAR SPINE (Language in Use boxes, book order):
1. **Past tense, regular and irregular** (U1, p.16) — the fullest
   past-simple treatment in the Egypt run: the past tells about
   actions that already happened; signal words listed (yesterday,
   last…, ago, in (2000), when I was young…); regular verbs add
   -ed; irregular verbs change their spelling (go/went,
   buy/bought); negative = didn't + base verb, with the book's
   own notice that the main verb stays in the base form after
   didn't; questions = Did + subject + base verb. A Tip box adds
   the three sounds of -ed: /ɪd/ (wanted, needed), /t/ (helped,
   looked), /d/ (called, cleaned).
2. **Conjunctions** (U2, p.32) — words that join parts of a
   sentence so ideas flow; and / but / or recalled from P3, and
   three new jobs stated: nor adds a second negative idea; so
   shows a result; because shows a reason or cause.
3. **Countable and uncountable nouns** (U3, p.48) — the fullest
   statement yet: countable nouns can be counted and take a, an,
   or numbers; uncountable nouns cannot be counted because they
   have no separate pieces, and take NO a/an/numbers — instead
   they are measured in container/piece expressions (a carton
   of, a piece of, slices of), the unit's packaging set.
4. **Adjectives** (U4, p.67) — words that describe nouns: they
   tell what something looks, feels, sounds, or smells like;
   sorted in print into describing people / objects / places /
   feelings and events. (Builds on P3 T2's naming and P4 T1's
   comparative/superlative rules.)
5. **Should / shouldn't for advice** (U5, p.83) — printed as
   sentence structures: subject + should + base verb;
   shouldn't = should not + base verb; questions open with
   Should + subject + base verb, with short answers (Yes, we
   should / No, you shouldn't).

PHONICS SPINE: silent letters (k, l) (U1, p.21) — k is not
pronounced before n at the start of a word (knock, knife, knee,
know); l is not pronounced in some words, especially after a or
o (walk, talk, half, folk, calm). **R-controlled words completed**
(U2, p.36): the letter r changes the sound of the vowel before
it — all five families now: ar, or, er, ir, ur (P3 T2 had taught
er/ar/or only). Silent letters (b, w) (U3, p.52) — b is hardly
heard after m at the end of a word (lamb, thumb, comb, climb);
w is not pronounced before r at the start (write, wrong, wrist,
wrap). Plural final -s sounds (U4, p.71): the same three-way
split as the -ed tip — /s/ (guests, books), /z/ (ribbons,
candles), /ɪz/ (houses, boxes) — with a Tip restating that a
singular subject adds -s to the verb and a plural subject does
not. oi / oy (U5, p.87): both spell the SAME sound; oi usually
sits in the middle of a word (coin, boil, point, noise) and oy
usually at the end (boy, toy, enjoy, joy) — the word's position
chooses the spelling; a Tip defines rhyming words on the same
spread.

UNITS, briefly: U1 — environment words by real photo (city,
village, desert, oasis, river); a dialog comparing city, village
and desert life in the past tense; two past-tense reading texts
(a village visit; a Nile riverbank day with a grandfather);
presentation project. U2 — the 3Rs taught in a teacher dialog
(reduce = use less, reuse = use again, recycle = turn old into
new); a go-green text (emails over paper letters, tote bags,
litter in the bin); a saving-resources text (water, electricity,
gas used wisely so they don't run out); brochure project. U3 —
a helping-others narrative (two children pack and deliver food
packages, help an elderly neighbor); a picnic dialog built
entirely out of packaging expressions (a carton of milk, a bar
of chocolate, a pack of, a bottle of, a slice of, a piece of, a
kilo of); an Al Azhar Park picnic text; poster project. U4 —
celebration phrase set (exchange gifts, perform music, play a
game, eat special food, socialize); a family-gathering dialog
(uncle plays the oud); an end-of-year party text loaded with
adjectives; a birthday text; thank-you-card project. U5 — real
places of Egypt by text and photo: Oyoun Mousa (Moses Springs,
South Sinai), a Dahab vacation dialog (diving, corals are living
animals — don't touch), Port Said and Port Fouad by ferry at
the Suez Canal; postcard project and a sites mind map. U6 —
the story unit (below).

WRITING SPINE: the **friendly letter** gets a printed five-part
checklist (U1, p.22): greeting (Dear + name), introduction (say
why you are writing), body (details or stories), closing (end
politely), signature — with a full model letter and a word bank
(time words, feeling words, action words, closings). The
**email** is modeled with its parts labeled on the message
itself (U2, p.37): title (subject), greeting, introduction
(what the email is about), body details, conclusion (a friendly
closing line), closing + name. The **narrative paragraph** gets
a labeled frame (U3, pp.54–55): Title; Beginning (when? who?
where?); Middle (what happened?); Ending (how did it finish?
how do you feel?) — worked on a model text, then set as the
learner's own frame. The **blog** frame returns from Term 1,
restated as six labeled parts on a model Labor Day post (U4,
p.72): title, your name, introduction, body with details,
conclusion, add a picture; the practice text is about Sinai
Liberation Day. The **postcard** is modeled whole (U5, p.89):
greeting, past-tense holiday news with advice, "Wish you were
here!", closing + name, address side with name and country,
and a real photo of the place (Dahab's Blue Hole).

STORY: "The Lost Kite" (U6, pp.94–103) is an ORIGINAL story
written for this book, filed by summary only: two friends fly a
new red kite in a field by their river village; the wind carries
it across the river onto a stranger's roof; rather than give
up, they cross the bridge, knock, and ask — and the kind old
man fetches it down for them. Before the story, a printed
Story Elements table (p.95) defines SIX parts — Title (the
name of the story), Setting (where and when), Characters
(people in the story), Plot/Problem (what happens, beginning
to end), Solution (what happens to fix the problem), Moral
(the lesson or message) — the fullest story-parts set in the
Egypt run, and the unit's work runs on it (fill the table,
order the events, write the problem and the solution, retell).
Core value: teamwork; life skill: kindness.

BOOK SLIPS, as printed: the U4 family-gathering dialog (p.63)
prints "evetn" for "even" and "his otud" for "his oud". The
Glossary (p.110) carries an entry labeled "lowercase: reuse"
defined as making new things from old materials (the recycle
definition), immediately followed by a correct "reuse" entry.
The back page's print spec claims 116 pages against the PDF's
112, and prints no ISBN or deposit number (Term 1 printed both).

five families + the oi/oy position rule), two-letter-sounds
sources (silent letters k/l and b/w).

## Egypt — English Primary 5 Term 1 (read 2026-10-25)

Source: the ministry e-learning host's 2026_2027 path — the
NEWEST of two live copies (the 2026-path copy is dated a year
older; the edition rule took the newer). Working file
`English_language_prim5_t1.pdf`, 18,757,051 bytes, ends %%EOF,
113 pages, and — a first since Primary 2 Term 2 — a REAL text
layer, so the book was read from its text with the rendered
pages as check. Printed folio = PDF page throughout.

Identity: ministry dedication (p.2); ministry message (p.6)
names the "Primary 5 Framework for Egyptian Learners." Back
page (p.112): developed/revised/supervised by the same team
(Profs. El-Naggar, El-Sayed, Younis; Dr. Mai Kamal; Ms. Amira
Awaad; CACD names; Dr. Akram Hassan Mohammed); Selah El
Telmeez; ISBN 978-633-8475-01-7; deposit no. 2026/8933; book
code 10/5/11/1/26/79; Arabic print spec says 116 pages (the
PDF holds 113); printing 2026–2027.

Shape: Scope and Sequence pp.3–5. Six units in the same
five-lesson rhythm, **but the self-assessments change shape
again**: back to the per-unit "Now I can…" list with the
three-face scale (I got it / I'm not sure / I need help), and
U1's even names the sounds and example sentences to be
checked. Review 1 after U3 (pp.57–60), Review 2 after U6
(pp.106–108); Glossary pp.109–111 BY CATEGORY — and its
categories now include Literature and Writing Types, defining
the book's own frames (plot, climax, fiction, non-fiction,
first-person and object-perspective narrative, story summary).
Unit spans: U1 Food, Nature, and Culture pp.7–24 · U2 My
Healthy Body pp.25–40 · U3 When Nature Changes pp.41–56 ·
U4 My Community pp.61–76 · U5 Our World, Our Resources
pp.77–91 · U6 The Talking Earth pp.92–105 (a literature unit).

GRAMMAR SPINE (boxes, book order):
1. **Adverbs of frequency** (U1, p.12) — how often something
   happens, taught as a PERCENTAGE LADDER: always 100%,
   usually 90%, often 70%, sometimes 50%, rarely 20%, never
   0%; position rule: before the main verb, after the verb
   "to be." First frequency teaching in the Egypt run.
2. **a / an / some / any** (U2, p.30) — a before a consonant
   SOUND, an before a vowel sound (singular countable nouns);
   some with plural countable or uncountable nouns in
   affirmative sentences; any with the same in negative
   sentences and questions. Completes the line that ran
   P3 countable/uncountable → P4 the container expressions.
3. **Past simple, the complete system** (U3, p.47) — finished
   actions or events; negatives didn't + infinitive, BUT with
   "to be" the not goes after was/were; questions Did …? and
   Wh-word + did …?, and with "to be" Was/Were …? and Wh-word
   + was/were …? — the P4 past box plus the to-be split and
   the Wh- forms, all stated in print.
4. **Prepositions of time** (U4, p.67) — at with clock times,
   mealtimes, and set phrases (at night); in with parts of the
   day, months, seasons, years; on with days and dates. (The
   place-preposition line stays where P4 left it; time gets
   its own box here.)
5. **Comparative adjectives completed** (U5, p.83) — P4's
   rules restated (short + -er + than; -y → -i + -er) and two
   things printed for the first time: long adjectives take
   "more / less" before the adjective + than, and an
   IRREGULAR table: good → better, bad → worse, little →
   less, many/much → more.
6. **Prefixes grow** (U5): dis- returns, joined by bi- and
   mis-, worked as pronunciation (the stressed syllable in
   prefixed words) per the Scope and the unit self-assessment.

PHONICS SPINE: **consonant clusters** (U1, p.16) — two or
more consonant sounds with no vowel between them, at the
beginning, middle, or end of a word (str-, bl-; tree,
branches, crocodiles, spring) — the general statement of
what P4 taught as blends. **The syllable** (U2, p.33) — a
word part with ONE vowel sound (letters don't decide it:
bread and pot are both one syllable), with a clap-it-out tip.
**Vowel digraphs** (U3, p.51) — two vowels together making
just one sound (ea in leap = long e; oo in flood = short u;
oa in boat = long o). **Trigraphs** (U4, p.69) — three
letters, one sound: dge says "j" (bridge), tch says "ch"
(match), igh says "i" (right).

UNITS, briefly: U1 — Nile life (what each river animal eats;
protect the river), desert animals, a Koshari recipe as the
writing model (ingredients list + sequence words: first,
then, next, after that, finally), and the restaurant flyer
project (the P4 leaflet parts restated: main heading,
sub-heading, image, menu, call to action). U2 — a sports-and-
health dialog (fit, mental health, stressed, lungs, muscles),
a then-and-now habits text (an early, unnamed taste of used
to), an informational heart-and-blood text (the heart is
about the size of your fist; vessels carry oxygen and take
away waste), healthcare workers as everyday heroes, a
healthy-habits poster (its elements stated: clear title,
organized steps, photos or drawings). U3 — extreme weather
words with printed definitions (thunderstorm, sandstorm,
flood, drought, wildfire, heat wave), a flood/drought/wildfire
news dialog, an ancient-Egypt weather text (canals and
basins, irrigation, mud-brick houses, the Khamsin), a fiction
writing lesson (model: a girl whispers upside-down clouds
right side up), collage project. U4 — community places,
village life vs the New Administrative Capital (Iconic Tower
facts: 385 m, 80 floors), a surprise full MATH lesson inside
English (two-digit × one-digit multiplication set out in
words: ones first, then tens), narrative writing. U5 —
Egypt's natural resources, a metals-and-minerals text
(non-renewable stated plainly: millions of years to form),
conservation talk, a "My Gold" poster (what is valuable in
your life). U6 — the literature unit (below).

WRITING SPINE: the **recipe** joins the writing kinds (U1) —
ingredients list first, then steps carried by sequence words,
with a writer's checklist. The **non-fiction tip returns**
(U2, p.36), restating P4's checks (is it true? did I make
anything up? is my source reliable?) with the frame Title /
Introduction / Body / Conclusion. **Fiction is defined**
(U3, p.52): a made-up story from the imagination, not based
on real things — and the **plot arc gains its fifth part**:
Beginning (meet the characters and setting) → Problem →
Middle (the character tries to fix it) → **Climax — the most
exciting part** → Ending (the problem is solved). The
**first-person narrative** gets its frame (U4, p.71): use I /
me / my; setting; a goal; description through the five senses
and adjectives; an ending — and the book's own statement of
why: a good story is one readers can see in their minds.
The **object-perspective narrative** is named as a kind
(U5, p.88): a non-living thing speaks in the first person —
what are you, description through the senses, ending with
why you are important (models: "I Am Water," "I Am Gold" —
filed by title only). U6's writing task: rewrite the story
from the heroine's perspective.

STORIES, by summary only: "The Giving Tree" (U1) — an
adaptation of Shel Silverstein's published story, softened:
a boy takes everything from an apple tree and never gives
back; years later he returns, finds it weak, and learns to
care for it; the printed moral is to appreciate those who
care for us, and the lesson diagrams it as Problem /
Solution / Result. The two frogs (U3) — the Aesop-type
fable: two frogs leave their drying pond, find a deep well,
and refuse it because a well that dries would trap them;
think before you decide. "Just Watch!" (U5) — an original
object-perspective story told by a little digger who frees
a stuck proud ship when the big machines cannot; size
doesn't matter, using your strength to help does. "The
Talking Earth" (U6) — credited on the unit opener to Jean
Craighead George; an adapted literary text filed by summary
only: Billie Wind, a Native American girl who wants to be
a scientist and does not believe the Earth can talk, is sent
alone into the Everglades and learns from the animals that
the Earth speaks through wind, water, and living things.

BOOK SLIPS, as printed: the Scope table spells "muscels"
for muscles (U2) and "Naitonal Park" for National Park (U6).
U3's questions ask why farmers can't grow food "in the US,"
but the dialog put the drought in China and the wildfire in
the US — the question mixes the two. Print spec says 116
pages against the PDF's 113.

Filed: blends sources (consonant clusters — the general
statement), two-letter-sounds sources (vowel digraphs +
trigraphs).

## Primary 5 Term 2 (read 2026-10-26)

Source: the MoE e-learning host, the one live copy —
2026/Primary/Primary5/Term2/StudentBook/English_Prim5_Tr2.pdf
(7,084,870 bytes, last-modified 2026-01-26; both 2026_2027

(red Scope table, star self-assessments), not Primary 5

Identity: cover "English / Primary 5 / Term 2 / 2025/2026"
(p.1); ministry dedication + © 2025 MoE (p.2); ministry

Shape: Scope and Sequence pp.4–5 (columns: Unit /

Skill(s) and Value(s) / Core values / Integrated Learning
Activities). Six units in the same five-lesson rhythm

Self-Assessment (I got it ★★★ / I'm not sure ★★ / I need

(pp.111–113); Glossary pp.114–119, ALPHABETICAL, ~130
words, one-line definition each. Unit spans: U1 "Hobbies
Make Us Shine!" pp.6–24 · U2 At the Doctor's pp.25–38 ·
U3 Suitcase Stories pp.39–55 · U4 Jobs in the Animal
Kingdom pp.59–76 · U5 Our Solar System pp.77–96 · U6
Offline but Happy pp.97–110 (a story unit).

### Grammar spine (Language in Use boxes, book order)

1. EXPRESSING ABILITY (U1, p.14) — can shows what someone
is able to do NOW (subject + can + verb; negative cannot /
can't); could shows what someone was able to do in the
PAST (subject + could + verb; negative could not /
couldn't). Completes Primary 3 Term 2's can/can't with
the past pair.
2. HAVE TO / DON'T HAVE TO vs MUST / MUSTN'T (U2, p.30) —
"we use these to talk about rules or things we should do":
have to = something necessary; don't have to = something
NOT necessary; must = a very important (strong) rule;
mustn't = not allowed. The don't-have-to / mustn't split
is stated in print — the obligation set is complete
(Primary 3 Term 2 had must/mustn't alone).
3. PLANNED FUTURE — GOING TO (U3, p.46) — "we use 'going
to' when we talk about something we plan to do in the
future": am with I, is with he/she/it, are with you/we/
they; affirmative subject + am/is/are + going to + verb;
negative am/is/are not + going to + verb; question Am/Is/
Are + subject + going to + verb? Key words: tomorrow,
next (week / month / year), soon, in the future, later,
in a few minutes (hours / days), one day. Stands beside
Primary 3 Term 2's will/won't future.
4. PRESENT SIMPLE vs PRESENT CONTINUOUS (U4, pp.66–67) —
the two set against each other in one box, the fullest
tense contrast in the Egypt run. Present Simple is "for
habits, facts, and jobs" — things always true or that
happen regularly; key words: every day, always, usually,
often, on Mondays; forms: I/you/we/they + base verb,
he/she/it + verb-s; negative don't / doesn't; questions
Do / Does. Present Continuous is "for actions happening
now" — happening at this moment; key words: now, right
now, at the moment, today, Look!, Listen!; forms am/is/
are + verb-ing in affirmative, negative (isn't / aren't /
'm not), and questions. Builds on Primary 3 Term 1's
continuous-alone box.
5. COMPARATIVES AND SUPERLATIVES TOGETHER (U5, p.84) —
comparatives show how two things are different;
superlatives show one thing is the most in a group of
three or more. Short adjectives: add -er + than / the +
-est, with doubling (big → bigger / biggest, hot →
hotter / hottest, small → smaller / smallest). Long
adjectives (two or more syllables): more ___ than / the
most ___. Special Tip irregulars: good–better–best,
bad–worse–worst, far–farther–farthest. Completes Primary
5 Term 1's comparative box — NOTE the small disagreement,
recorded as printed in both: Term 1 called long adjectives
"3+ syllables" and printed "less" comparatives; Term 2
says two or more syllables and works more / the most.

### Word-building spine (this book's "Phonetics Skills")

- SUFFIX defined (U1, p.18, Word Building Skills): a
suffix is a group of letters added to the END of a word to
change its meaning or make a new word; -ment changes a
verb into a noun (enjoy → enjoyment); -ship changes a
noun into another noun (friend → friendship).
Pronunciation rule: the stress stays on the main word,
not on -ment or -ship. The suffix counterpart to the
prefix line (Primary 4 Term 1 dis-/il-/ir-; Primary 5
Term 1 dis-/bi-/mis-).
- SUFFIXES -able / -ible (U2, p.33): added to the end of
a word to form an adjective meaning "able to"
(comfortable = able to feel relaxed, curable = able to be
cured, accessible = easy to reach); -able is pronounced
/əb(ə)l/, -ible /ɪb(ə)l/; stress is usually on the first
syllable of the base word.
- ADVERBS ENDING IN -ly (U3, p.50): "an adverb tells us
how we do something"; make them by adding -ly to
adjectives (quick → quickly, loud → loudly, careful →
carefully); y changes to i before -ly (happy → happily);
the ending is pronounced /li/. First adverb-FORMATION
teaching in the run (Primary 3 Term 2 named adverbs of
sequence only).
- SUFFIXES -er / -or / -ist (U4, p.71): person and job
endings — -er sounds /ər/ at the end, soft, with the
strong part before it (farmer); -or also sounds /ər/
(visitor); -ist sounds /ɪst/ (specialist); clap-the-
stressed-syllable practice.
- COMPOUND WORDS (U5, p.89): a compound word is made when
two small words are joined together to make one new word
(class + room = classroom, sun + light = sunlight, foot
+ ball = football); sometimes written as one word
(classroom), sometimes as two words (ice cream).

### Writing spine

- STEPS TEXT + SEQUENCING WORDS COMPLETED (U1 L4, p.20):
the sequence set with each word's job stated — First =
the beginning step, Next = the second step, Then = the
mid steps, After that = more mid steps, Finally = the
last step; the writing checklist itself is written in
can/could ("I can see my title… I could add more
details… I could read it aloud to check it flows").
- SURVEY defined in print (U1 L5): "a survey is a group
of questions that people answer to share their ideas,
opinions, or experiences"; the project is a seven-question
hobbies survey with checkboxes and one open question.
- FORMAL EMAIL (U2 L4, p.34): the email frame grown
formal, every part named — From (the person sending),
To (the person receiving), Subject (the subject line
tells what the email is about), Greeting ("Dear" + the
person's name), reason for writing and short explanation,
explain how long the leave will be and when you'll come
back, a polite closing sentence, a formal closing (your
full name at the end). Model: a sick-leave email; Review
1's writing task repeats the same task.
- INFORMAL EMAIL INVITATION (U3 L4, p.51): Guided Writing
Steps — Greeting (start with "Dear [Name]," and a
friendly opening), Invitation, Describe Places (pick 2–3
landmarks, 1–2 sentences each), Closing (ask for a reply
and sign your name); with its own writing checklist. The
formal / informal email pair is now complete.
- DESCRIPTIVE WRITING TIP (U4 L4, p.73): 1 choose a
topic; 2 use strong adjectives; 3 SHOW, DON'T TELL —
named in print, with the book's own contrast (instead of
saying the egret is helpful, write it catching harmful
insects in the rice field); 4 use the correct tense
(Present Simple for habits/jobs, Present Continuous for
what is happening now); 5 organize your ideas: start with
what you see, describe the job, finish with your opinion.
- FACT FILE (U5 L5): a new writing kind — a model Milky
Way fact file with headed sections (What is it? / What's
Inside? / Where Are We? / Amazing Facts), then make your
own: introduce your planet, what you can see, what you
can do, how you will feel; use adjectives; include at
least one compound word.
- LISTENING TIP (U5 L1, p.81): good listeners pay
attention to key words (names, numbers, places), main
ideas (what the speaker is mostly talking about), and
details (facts that give extra information) — "you don't
need to understand every single word to get the meaning."

### Units, briefly

U1 — hobby words by picture (board games, cycling,
skating, painting, singing, solving puzzles, playing the
piano, crafting with paper); talent-show dialog; "Hobbies
Really Count!" text (hobbies help us relax, have fun, stay
healthy; skills grow — can now vs couldn't before); the
School Hobby Club story (each hobby gets its own day —
the -ment/-ship words live inside it: arrangement,
excitement, agreement, friendship); survey project.
U2 — clinic dialog (Fady's check-up; Tip p.26: we use
have/has to talk about how we feel when we are sick —
have with I/you/we/they, has with he/she/it); "The First
Aid Kit at School" text (bandages, plasters, scissors;
must tell a teacher; mustn't open the kit without
permission); "Hospital 57-3-57: A Place of Hope" — the
REAL Cairo children's cancer hospital: all treatment free,
funded by people's donations, with playrooms, libraries,
and teachers so children keep studying (real institution,
real photo); formal sick-leave email; Healthy Hero
Bookmark project (health tips written as imperatives).
U3 — travel words by real photo (suitcase, pack, travel,
crowded, beach, ticket, each with a printed meaning);
Alexandria dialog (Qaitbay Citadel, Bibliotheca
Alexandrina); "A Special Trip Home" text (an Egyptian
family living abroad tours home: Pyramids and Sphinx,
train to Luxor's temples, Nile cruise and felucca to
Aswan, Alexandria beach); camping story (Wadi El Gemal
Park — tents, campfire, nature trail, clean the campsite
before leaving); email invitation; Travel Adventure Book
project.
U4 — animal jobs dialog (police dogs find lost people and
catch criminals with their noses; guide dogs help blind
people walk safely; horses calm people; rescue dolphins);
Egyptian animal helpers by REAL PHOTO with printed
meanings (water buffalo pulls ploughs, donkey carries
loads, pigeon carries messages home, falcon hunts crop
pests, gecko eats insects in houses, scarab beetle works
the soil); farm text (cows, sheep and wool, guard dog,
chickens, the cat catching mice); "The Farmer's
Feathered Friend" — egrets eat the insects that harm
crops and ride on buffaloes' backs, so farmers need fewer
chemicals; poster project (be kind to animal helpers).
U5 — planetarium dialog (the planets in order, Mars the
Red Planet for its iron-rich soil, Jupiter the largest,
Saturn's rings, the Milky Way our galaxy); "Egypt's
First Astronaut" — a real-person article about Sara
Sabry (first Egyptian female astronaut, first Arab
engineer to travel into space, 2022; real photos) whose
planet comparisons carry the unit's grammar; "The Planet
Project" story (Adam will not share the poster work until
it fails; teamwork moral); "My Trip in a Spaceship" dream
text for descriptive writing; Milky Way fact file (over
200 billion stars; light takes about 100,000 years to
cross it; the Sun takes about 230 million years to go
once around it); Space Travel Brochure project.
U6 — the story unit (below).

### The story — by summary only

"Offline but Happy" (U6, pp.98–110) is an ORIGINAL story
written for this book. Vocabulary first, with printed
definitions (pp.98–99), and the four characters named
(Tarek, Amir, Laila, Dina). Plot summary: on a Friday
morning Tarek's Wi-Fi is down, so his tablet game will
not load; bored and lonely, he first refuses his friends'
call, then joins Laila, Amir, and Dina outside — chalk
drawings on the sidewalk, a bike race to the park,
hide-and-seek, a pretend shop built from leaves and
sticks. When his mother calls that the Wi-Fi is back, he
chooses to stay and play. From then on he uses the tablet
only a little, after time with friends. The printed moral
(the unit's self-assessment states it): no tablet, phone,
or screen can replace the joy of friendship. The unit's
story work runs on the SIX-part story-elements frame —
Main Characters / Setting / Problem / Solution / Ending /
Moral (p.107) — the same set as Primary 4 Term 2's printed
table; closing task: write a letter to Tarek giving him
advice about friends and screens.

BOOK SLIPS, as printed: the ministry message (p.3) drops
letters inside a few words ("tailor nstruction" among
them); the U3 camping story prints "look fo: special
birds" (for "for"); the U4 egret text prints "the 'ields"
(for "the fields"); the U6 story prints "something fun tc
do outside" (for "to") and "at the minimal use"; Review
2's reading text prints "better fcr plants" (for "for").
The print spec (120 pages) matches the PDF exactly.

time), then a Glossary BY CATEGORY (pp.107–111, ~120
words). Unit spans: U1 Amazing Places in Egypt pp.6–19 ·
U2 Our Nature pp.20–35 · U3 Community Builders pp.36–49 ·
Review 1 pp.50–53 · U4 Resources Around Us pp.54–68 · U5
Made in Egypt pp.69–82 · two photo plates pp.83–84 · U6
The Water Savers pp.85–93 · Review 2 pp.94–97 ·
Self-Assessments pp.98–100 · Audio Scripts pp.101–106 ·
Glossary pp.107–111 · back page p.112.

GRAMMAR SPINE (Language in Use boxes, book order). This
book mostly RESTATES the system's grammar at review level
— and states several of them in their fullest form yet:
1. Present Simple (U1, p.13) — used to talk about habits
   and routines; worked through examples and form
   exercises only (no new rule table).
2. Present Continuous (U2, p.26) — actions happening now;
   negative = subject + am/is/are + not + verb-ing;
   questions = am/is/are + subject + verb-ing, and
   Wh-word + am/is/are + subject + verb-ing. (P5 T2's
   contrast box had set it against the simple; here it
   stands alone with its full form table.)
3. Can / Can't / Able to (U3, p.41) — can/can't + the
   infinitive form of the verb talks about ability in
   the present; be (am/is/are) + able to + infinitive
   shows the same ability. The able-to twin is new.
4. Modal Verbs Must / Mustn't / Should / Shouldn't (U4,
   p.60) — the fullest single modal box in the run, all
   four in one place, each with the infinitive rule:
   must + infinitive = very important or a rule;
   mustn't = must + not + infinitive = not allowed;
   should + infinitive = advice or a good idea;
   shouldn't = should + not + infinitive = not a good
   idea. With P5 T2's have to / don't have to, the
   obligation-and-advice system is now complete AND
   gathered.
5. Past Simple (U5, p.75) — the COMPLETE statement of
   the tense in the Egypt run: three uses printed —
   (a) actions that started and finished in the past,
   (b) actions that happened one after another, (c)
   habits in the past. Affirmative: subject + past form;
   regular verbs add -ed / -d / -ied (play→played,
   smile→smiled, cry→cried); irregular verbs change in
   different ways (go→went, eat→ate, buy→bought).
   Negative: didn't + infinitive. Question: Did +
   subject + main verb, and Wh- + did. Verb "to be" is
   split out in print: past forms was/were (negative
   wasn't/weren't), questions use was/were — "We don't
   use 'did' with verb to be."

PRONUNCIATION SPINE:
- The /ǝr/ sound (U1, p.15): built by filling the missing
  letters (b_ _ _d, st_ _ _ _, squ_ _ _ _); how to say it
  printed: open your mouth slightly, say "eh," then add
  the /r/ sound — one sound, as in "wear" or "stare."
- /e/ vs /ɪ/ (U2, p.30): both given articulation
  instructions in print — /e/: mouth slightly open,
  tongue low-mid and front (ten, hen, bed); /ɪ/: tongue
  high but relaxed, lips and jaw relaxed, tongue tip
  just behind the bottom front teeth (sit, bit, in).
- Syllables and word stress (U3, p.45): clap each
  syllable; the STRESS TIP defines stress in print —
  the part of the word we say louder and stronger; in
  one-syllable words the whole word is stressed (NURSE);
  in two-syllable words stress is usually on the FIRST
  syllable (TAI-lor); in some three-syllable words on
  the SECOND (me-CHAN-ic).
- Long /u:/ vs short /ʊ/ (U4, p.64): the oo pair of P3
  T2 restated, each with a "How to pronounce" panel
  (spoon, moon, school vs wood, book, look, good).
- The -ed three sounds — THE RULE STATED (U5, p.78):
  P4 T2 gave the tip with examples; this book prints
  the voicing rule itself: /t/ after voiceless sounds
  (p, k, f, s, ch, sh — liked, watched, kissed); /d/
  after vowels or voiced sounds (b, g, v, l, m, n, r, z
  — played, cleaned, called); /ɪd/ after t or d
  (wanted, needed).

UNITS, briefly. U1 — activity phrases by picture (buy
souvenirs, make furniture, play music, walk to a small
museum, visit ancient sites, take photos); a listening
day in Old Cairo (Khan El Khalili, the audio script is
in the back); the Ras Mohamed National Park text (coral
reefs, snorkel, landscape, marine life — real place,
protected area); village life across four villages
(Bashandi in the New Valley, Ezbet el Borg in Damietta,
Qutur in Gharbia, Shakhlouba in Kafr El Sheikh) with the
words accompany / looms / fishing net / dye DEFINED IN
PRINT as a matching exercise; Fady's informal email
about his school day + a Tip: an informal email goes to
a friend or someone you know very well, and you use the
present simple to talk about your routine; poster
project (daily life in a governorate — parts named:
main header, detailed description, pictures/drawings).
U2 — caring for plants (Grandpa's garden dialog: natural
fertilizer, weeds, hibiscus, basil; a Listening Tip:
listen carefully for main ideas and important details);
the weather email (Alexandria windy/rainy, Cairo warm/
sunny, Aswan hot — the present continuous at work in a
real text); The Wonderful Nile River (about 6,650 km,
longest river in the world, flows south to north;
tilapia, Nile perch, crocodiles, kingfishers); a
SKIMMING TIP (reading quickly to get the main idea —
focus on the most important parts); the Wadi El Rayan /
Wadi El-Hitan school-trip story; story-writing steps +
checklist (below). U3 — jobs that help the community by
picture (paramedic, architect, water resources engineer,
mechanic, tailor, delivery person, street cleaner,
traffic officer) with six words defined in print
(community, research, vehicles, emergency, brave,
control); a solve-it listening (traffic near the school;
posters; "Drive Slowly!" signs); The Proud Rose story +
a Moral Tip (below); the biography of Dr. Mostafa
El-Sayed (born 1933, Ain Shams then the USA, chemistry
and nanoscience, El-Sayed's Rule, gold nanoparticles
against cancer, US National Medal of Science) + seven
biography-writing tips; the community-problem project
(a Food Waste in Egypt chart worked as Causes / Effects
/ Solutions). (Review 1 adds: "We call 123 in case of
emergency" — Egypt's ambulance number; P5 T2's safety
unit gave police 122.) U4 — ancient materials by picture
(granite, colored stones, gold, clay, papyrus, bricks,
temple, amulet) with six words defined in print
(material, survive, ancient, decorate, wrapped, modern);
the Let's Use Energy Wisely text (solar/wind clean; oil
and gas harm; small saving actions); Gifts from Under
the Ground (minerals defined as the valuable things
under the ground; oil in the Western Desert and Sinai,
natural gas, gold and iron in the Eastern Desert, steel);
Man-Made Resources (Omar's research text: plastic from
oil, glass from sand, bricks from clay and water, paper
from trees) + RESEARCH TIPS (below); the High Dam fact
file (fact file returns — headed question sections:
What is it? / Why did they build it? / When? / How does
it help? — built 1960–1970, Lake Nasser) with six
fact-file steps (ask and answer questions, find key
facts, write short sentences, add title and
sub-headings, include pictures, check spelling and
grammar). U5 — travel words by picture (passport,
luggage, souvenir, brochure, travel plan, exhibition,
guided tour, information desk) + the travel verbs fly /
pack / book / board; the Grand Egyptian Museum listening
(near the Pyramids at Giza, almost 20 years to build,
the Solar Boat of King Khufu, smart screens); Al Galala
City text (a modern smart city above the clouds near
Ain Sokhna, planned 2015, cable car, Galala University);
The Bundle of Sticks fable + story-elements work
(below); Mariam's Benban Solar Park article + OPINION
WRITING (below). U6 — the story unit (below).

STORY / WRITING SPINE:
- Moral Tip restated with its fullest wording (U3,
  p.42): "Every story has a moral. A moral is the
  lesson or message we learn from a story. It usually
  teaches us the difference between right and wrong,
  and helps us think about how we should act in real
  life."
- Story-writing steps (U2, p.33): tell when and where
  the story takes place; say who is in the story; write
  what happens using first, next, and finally; include
  a problem and a solution; use past simple tense; add
  interesting details; finish with an ending. Checklist:
  say who / tell when and where / find information
  about the place / friendly style / interesting
  details / an ending that wraps up the story.
- Research Tips (U4, p.66): choose your topic; ask
  simple questions (What is it? Where does it come
  from?); use different sources (school book, safe
  websites, ask an adult); write short notes IN YOUR
  OWN WORDS; organize your ideas; add pictures or
  drawings; check spelling, grammar, and punctuation.
  The follow-up checklist makes the learner REJECT the
  wrong steps: copy notes from the internet, use only
  one resource, write long notes, unfriendly style.
- Story elements, six names again (U5, p.76):
  beginning / middle / end / moral / characters /
  setting — each with a one-line printed definition
  (setting = the place and time of a story; middle =
  where the problem or main event happens; moral = the
  lesson we learn). A reading Tip beside it: when you
  read a story, try to feel what the character feels.
- OPINION WRITING defined and taken apart (U5, pp.79–
  80): the Tip defines it — writing about what you
  think or how you feel about something is called
  opinion writing, a way to share your ideas and say
  what you believe. A fact/opinion sorting exercise
  (F = true and can be proven; O = what someone
  believes). The features of opinion writing are named
  as a matching set: opinion starter (I think…), 
  supporting reasons, use of examples, sequence words
  (First…, Second…, Third…, As a result…, In
  conclusion…), strong adjectives, and the writer's
  voice using 1st person pronouns.
- The Glossary's Writing Types table (pp.110–111)
  defines the narrative kinds as a system: personal
  narrative (a real event from the writer's own life;
  first person; reflection), descriptive writing
  (sensory details; imagery), fictional narrative
  (made-up; character, plot, climax — the most
  exciting or important moment — and RESOLUTION: how
  the main problem is solved — the plot arc gains its
  sixth part), biography (a true story about someone's
  life written by another person; timeline; factual
  details), informational text, object perspective
  (personification; point of view; imaginative voice),
  and problem-solution (cause, effect, solution,
  sequence). The same Glossary defines paragraph,
  main idea and details (under "Display Board"), and
  carries a Suffixes category of -er person words
  (farmer, helper, cleaner, baker, builder) — the
  suffix line of P5 T2 surfacing in the word list;
  U4's self-assessment likewise claims "add prefixes
  and suffixes to words."

STORIES, by summary only:
- "The Proud Rose" (U3, pp.42–44) — the classic fable,
  retold: a beautiful, proud rose mocks the rough,
  thorn-covered cactus beside her; in a drought summer
  she dries and thirsts, watches a bird drink water
  stored inside the cactus, apologizes, and is saved
  by his shared water; she never judges by looks
  again. (The book's words for the plants are defined
  first: thorns, petals, peck, unpleasant.)
- "The Bundle of Sticks" (U5, pp.76–77) — the Aesop
  fable: a farmer's three sons argue constantly; the
  old man has each try to break a tied bundle (they
  can't), then single sticks (easily broken); "Unity
  gives strength." The unit sorts its parts into
  Beginning / Middle / End / Characters / Setting and
  the Quick Review writes another ending for it.
- "The Water Savers" (U6, pp.86–93) — an ORIGINAL
  story written for this book, told whole as the
  unit. Vocabulary first, with printed definitions
  (drip, overflow, puddle, leak, hallway, hose, tap,
  pipe, drop; dripping, precious, resources, snap her
  fingers, curious, leaky, cheerful, fix, spread,
  mini-challenges), and the characters named up front:
  Noor, Ahmed, Farida, and Mr. Youssef the school
  cleaner. Plot summary: three friends at a school
  near Luxor notice a bathroom tap dripping and a
  broken pipe overflowing into puddles; they audit
  the school (notebook, camera, curious eyes), make
  posters ("Turn off the tap while brushing!" "Every
  drop counts. Fix leaks fast!"), learn from the
  cleaner how a small drip wastes liters a day, stand
  up to a classmate who mocks them ("Because water is
  life"), and see their message spread — posters in
  bathrooms, classrooms and near garden taps, daily
  teacher talks, class mini-challenges — until the
  head teacher gives them certificates of achievement
  and the whole school becomes Water Savers. The
  fourth whole-story unit in a row works the
  story-elements frame (Review 2: write beginning /
  middle / end / moral / characters / setting for it
  in your notebook).

GRAMMAR SPINE (boxes, book order) — where Term 1
restated the system, Term 2 ADDS FIVE NEW GRAMMAR
THINGS, the biggest single-term grammar jump in the
Primary run:
1. Conjunction Pairs — correlative conjunctions (U1,
   p.12): either…or talks about a choice between two
   things; neither…nor says two things are not true;
   both…and says two things are true at the same time.
   The U1 reading text is built out of them.
2. Relative Pronouns (U2, p.30): defined in print —
   relative pronouns are words that join two sentences
   together. who = people (the person doing an action);
   whose = possession; which = things and animals;
   that = things and ideas, adding information that is
   important.
3. Present Perfect (U3, p.47): the run's first new
   tense since the present continuous. Form: has/have +
   past participle. Uses printed: actions that started
   in the past and continue now; things that happened
   recently with results we can see today. Full
   structure table (affirmative / negative has-have +
   not + participle / question Has-Have + subject +
   participle), a Tip (has with he/she/it, have with
   I/you/we/they), regular participles add -ed,
   irregulars change form (build→built, go→gone,
   write→written). Both U3 reading texts are written in
   it before the box arrives.
4. Used to / Didn't use to / Did … use to (U4, p.67):
   used to + base verb for things that happened
   regularly in the past but not now — past habits or
   states no longer true; after "used to" the verb is
   always in the base form; didn't use to + base for
   things that didn't happen in the past; Did + subject
   + use to + base verb? for questions. Full Form / Use
   / Example table. (The unnamed used-to text of
   Primary 5 Term 1 now gets its named grammar.)
5. First Conditional (U5, p.81): used to talk about
   possible situations in the future — "If this happens
   now, then that will happen in the future." Form:
   If + present simple, will + base form of the verb;
   the if-clause is the condition (present simple), the
   main clause is the result (will + verb).
6. Past Continuous — NAMED but NOT BOXED: the Scope
   and Sequence lists U6's language as "Past Simple –
   Past Continuous," and the U6 story's telling mixes
   them ("were helping their teacher clean," "the
   fountain wasn't working"), but no rule box for the
   past continuous is printed anywhere in the book.
   Recorded as the book's one promised-but-untaught
   grammar item.

WORD-BUILDING SPINE (this term's second spine):
- Prefixes re- and un- (U2, p.34): the prefix definition
  restated (a small part added to the beginning of a
  word; it changes the meaning and sometimes the
  sound). re- = again or back (rebuild, rewrite,
  recycle, return); un- = not or the opposite
  (unhappy, unsafe, unfair, unlock). Two pronunciation
  tips: re- sounds "ree" before a strong syllable
  (rebuild) but "ri" before a weak one (return); the
  stress usually stays on the main word, not the
  prefix (unHAPpy). The Quick Review sorts true re-
  words from words that merely start with "re" (rain,
  read, rest, real).
- Suffix -ify and -er (U3, p.51): -ify means "to make"
  or "to cause to become," added to nouns or adjectives
  to form verbs (simple→simplify, pure→purify,
  clear→clarify); -er means someone who performs an
  action (drive→driver, bake→baker, teach→teacher).
- en– and –en, a matched pair (U4, p.70): en- at the
  beginning means to cause to be or to put into
  (enrich, enlarge, encourage, endanger, enable); -en
  at the end means to make or to become (widen,
  darken, lighten, shorten, soften, brighten) — the
  same "make" meaning added at either end of the word.

PRONUNCIATION SPINE:
- Word stress, the rules grown (U1, p.16): stress
  defined again (one part of a word said stronger and
  louder) — then the new layer: the SAME word can move
  its stress with its job: PREsent (noun, a gift) vs
  preSENT (verb, to give); REcord (noun) vs reCORD
  (verb). Two-syllable rules: nouns and adjectives
  stress the FIRST syllable (TAble, PICture, HAppy);
  verbs stress the SECOND (beGIN, deCIDE, reLAX).
  Three-syllable words ending in -er or -ly stress the
  first syllable (MANager, CLEVerly, HAPpily).
- Triphthongs (U5, p.85): a triphthong is a vowel sound
  that combines THREE vowel sounds together in one
  syllable — ow-er (hour, our, flower: start with "ow,"
  then glide to "er") and ay-er (layer, player, mayor).
  The letter/sound-group line completes its climb:
  digraph (P5 T1) → trigraph (P5 T1) → triphthong.

City descriptive model + four descriptive-paragraph

what it looks like; what people can do there; end with

metro words by picture with printed meanings

EMAIL whose parts are LABELED on the model (Clear
Subject / Greeting / Friendly Opening / Topic
Introduction / Main Idea / Details / Personal Opinion /

U6 — the story unit (below). Review 1 adds a

STORY, by summary only: "The Library That Time Forgot"
(U6, pp.92–103) is an ORIGINAL story written for this
book. Vocabulary first with printed definitions (dust,
storage, rusty, clinked, library, creak, stacks,
windmill, volunteers, town, clogged, pipe, fountain,
treasure); the characters named up front: Adam, Lara,
Ziad, Hana, Fady. A printed STORY ELEMENTS panel (p.95)
gives Setting / Characters / Plot / Problem / Solution
— the five-part working set (moral is asked in the
questions: "Working together and learning can help the
community"). Plot summary: five friends cleaning their
school's storage room find a large bronze key tagged
"Library"; it opens the town's forgotten library, full
of dusty books, old maps and travelers' letters; over a
week they learn from it — plants that clean water, a
small windmill built from a diagram, their town's
history; using a library guide they find why the town
fountain stopped (a clogged pipe) and fix it with a
plumber's help; the principal calls the library "a
treasure" that "should be open to everyone"; the town
cleans, repairs and reopens it, and the friends become
its first official volunteers. Closing line: "We
thought the key only opened a door, but it actually
opened our minds." Writing task: a diary entry from one
child's point of view, with printed guidelines (short
paragraph, past-tense verbs, describe your feelings and
what happened, end with what you learned) — the diary
returns from Primary 4 Term 1, now as a story-response
kind.

Identity: cover "ENGLISH — Preparatory Schools - Year
One - Term 1 — 2025 - 2026 — Student's Book" (p.1, new
design). Ministry dedication (p.2, now "to the students
and families"). Ministry message (p.4) is the
PREPARATORY variant — "the preparatory framework for
Egyptian learners," "Preparatory Stage students" — and
its Key Features list is new: age-appropriate vocabulary,
grammar and functions taught in context; focused
four-skills development; scaffolded writing tasks that
progress from guided to independent writing; vocabulary
reinforcement using visuals and themes; activities for
higher-order thinking, not just memorization. Back page
(p.112): "Preparatory One - Term 1, Academic Year:
2025/2026"; developed with the Content Department Team
at GPS Publishing and Printing Company (the Prep books
change publisher — the Primary run was Selah El
Telmeez); reviewed by Prof. Zeinab El-Naggar, Prof.
Attia El-Sayed, Dr. Mai Kamal, Mr. Reda Shalby, Mr.
Mohamed Gomaa, Mr. Mostafa Zaghlol, Mr. Ayman Elsayed;
CACD team as before; general supervision Dr. Akram
Hassan Mohammed; British Council + CACD + GPS marks;
print spec 112 pages (MATCHES), 27 × 19 cm, interior
70 g white paper, cover 180 g coated, 4 colors. NO ISBN
and NO deposit number printed.

A NEW FILE SHAPE: this PDF interleaves a TEACHER'S
section with the pupil book. Before the pupil Unit 1
(pp.5–12) sit: the Scope and Sequence (pp.5–7),
"Lesson components" explainer pages that tell the
teacher what each lesson part is for (Before you start /
Reading / Think about this / Vocabulary / Speaking /
Listening / Language in use / Word-building skills /
Story Time / Before you write / Class performance),
a preview of Unit 1's Roundtable and Assessment Corner,
and START SMART (printed pp.11–12) — a diagnostic
pre-test of the Primary system (a short conversation
dialog, present simple forms, a/an/some, opposites,
prepositions, word ordering across present simple and
continuous, and a paragraph task using present simple
AND present continuous). The pupil book proper runs
printed pp.13–103, then full AUDIOSCRIPTS (printed
pp.104–109, including Start Smart's) and two blank
Notes pages. There is NO glossary in this book —
vocabulary is defined inside the lessons.

PUPIL UNIT SHAPE (six lessons, every unit): L1 Reading
· L2 Listening + Language in Use · L3 Reading +
Word-building · L4 Story Time · L5 Writing · L6
Team-Project (Roundtable: Circle Sharing, Group
Discussion, a make-something Activity) — then an
Assessment Corner (Vocabulary / Language / Writing)
and a My Self-Reflection page ("I can…" by skill, plus
Life Skills & Values). Writing tasks are 80–100 words
throughout (the Primary books asked 24–70). Unit spans
(printed): U1 Life in a Digital World pp.13–26 · U2
Learning to Learn pp.27–40 · U3 Role Models pp.41–54 ·
Review 1 pp.55–58 · U4 Smart Choices pp.59–72 · U5
Future Dreams pp.73–86 · U6 Global Citizens pp.87–100 ·
Review 2 pp.101–103 · Audioscripts pp.104–109.

GRAMMAR: the term is a deliberate RE-TEACHING of the
whole Primary grammar system at Prep depth — every unit
restates one or two Primary things in Tip-sized boxes
with harder exercises (correct-the-mistake is the house
style now):
- U1: Present Simple (facts, habits, routine; the form
  table) + Adverbs of Frequency — the percentage ladder
  restated with ONE CHANGE from Primary 5 Term 1:
  rarely is 10% here (P5 T1 printed 20%); always 100,
  usually 90, often 70, sometimes 50, never 0. Position
  rule restated (before the main verb, after "be").
  PARTS OF SPEECH defined as a set: a noun names a
  person, thing, animal or place; a verb shows an
  action; an adjective describes nouns — the verb joins
  the noun/adjective definitions of the Primary books.
- U2: Can / Can't for ability (two short Tips) +
  Present Continuous (actions happening now; be +
  -ing) — both restated briefly.
- U3: Past Simple (Tip: actions that started and ended
  in the past; regular -ed; irregular not formed with
  -ed) + the verb-to-be table (I/he/she/it was;
  we/you/they were).
- U4: First Conditional (Tip: the probable result of
  an action; "if" or "when" + present simple, followed
  by will + infinitive; the clauses can swap) +
  Should / Shouldn't for advice.
- U5: Going to (Tip: be + going to + infinitive for
  future plans and future intentions — and a third use
  the Primary box didn't name: PREDICTION WITH
  EVIDENCE, "Look at the clouds! It's going to rain.").
- U6: Must / Mustn't (necessary to do / not allowed to
  do) + a "Review of tenses" Tip gathering present
  simple, present continuous, past simple and the
  going-to plan in four example sentences.

PUNCTUATION SPINE — the Prep book teaches punctuation
as its own line, one mark per unit, each with a printed
"Learn" box:
- U1 Capital Letters: the full rule set in one place —
  beginning of a sentence; proper nouns; days, months
  and holidays BUT NOT seasons; languages and
  nationalities; the pronoun "I."
- U2 Period & Question Mark: a period ends a statement;
  a question mark ends a question.
- U3 Comma: listing items; joining clauses with linking
  words; addressing people.
- U4 Exclamation Point: strong emotion or emphasis —
  surprise, excitement, anger, joy.
- U5 Apostrophe: possession (incl. the plural rule —
  noun plural ending in s takes the apostrophe after
  the s) and contractions.
- U6: a mixed practice page only.

WORD-BUILDING SPINE:
- Word families REDEFINED (U1): "All words belong to
  families… One type of word family involves words that
  come from the same ROOT word" — work → worker,
  homework, workbook, schoolwork; board → keyboard,
  smartboard, noticeboard, board game. (A different
  sense from the Primary rhyme families; both are the
  books' own uses of the same name — recorded, not
  merged.)
- The SPIDERGRAM defined (U2 Tip): a diagram that helps
  organize ideas — the main idea in the middle, the
  "legs" show supporting details or examples.
- Suffixes and prefixes gathered (U3): the suffix
  definition restated ("a letter or a group of letters
  that you add to the end of a word to make a new
  word") with a working table (-ful → adjective:
  power→powerful; -ist: tour→tourist; -ment:
  agree→agreement; -er: think→thinker); the prefix
  definition restated ("a group of letters we add to
  the beginning of a word to change its meaning");
  opposite-making prefixes un- / ir- / dis- / in-; and
  ROOT defined: "the base word before adding a prefix
  or suffix."
- Synonym and Antonym defined (U4 Tips): a synonym is
  a word with the same meaning (big → large); an
  antonym is a word with an opposite meaning
  (hot → cold).
- Collocations defined (U5 Word Tip): words that often
  go together naturally — verb + noun (score goals,
  have a job, match talent, build a machine) and
  adjective + noun (digital life, critical thinking).
- Connectors (U6 Word Tip): because shows cause, so
  shows result, although shows contrast.

THE STORY: the Prep year tells CHAPTERED STORIES — one
story per three units, a chapter in each unit's Story
Time, the same children throughout (Sara, Omar, Zain):
- "The Magic Classroom" (U1–U3): Ch.1 The First
  Challenge — the children enter a glowing classroom
  of floating puzzles; a locked box opens only by
  teamwork. Ch.2 The Power of Unity — an invisible
  bridge that appears under kind words and brave steps
  (respect, ambition, courage defined inside the story
  by the children themselves); a golden door opens
  when they sing together: "Determination opens every
  door." Ch.3 The Greatest Lessons — a glowing book
  names the values (Responsibility, Curiosity,
  Teamwork, Respect, Determination, Courage, and
  honesty — "doing the right thing even when no one
  was watching"); the real magic is the values, not
  the puzzles.
- "The Dream Team" (U4–U6; Lily and Karim join): Ch.1
  Never Give Up — the village invention fair; the team
  builds a water-saving machine, learning fairness when
  Omar's lead-taking leaves Lily out. Ch.2 — setbacks,
  no blame when Karim errs; Lily finds her voice. Ch.3
  A Team of Tomorrow — another team wins first prize;
  their teacher calls them "the real winners" — values
  over trophies; they become the village's Dream Team.
Filed by summary only.

TEXTS AND WRITING, briefly: U1 Tarek's blog "A Day in
My Digital Life" (frequency adverbs at work) + Joury's
devices email; writing = an email about your digital
routine (Helpful Hints: start Hi + name, say why you
are writing, short simple sentences, close with "Write
back soon!" / "Talk to you later!"). U2 learning styles
— a self-quiz sorts the learner as visual / auditory /
kinesthetic; writing = a learning-plan paragraph
(topic sentence, describing details, stick to one place
or event). U3 three real role models in past simple:
Dr. Samira Moussa (physicist; "My wish for cancer
treatment is to be as cheap as aspirin" — the book's
one quoted line, kept short), Dr. Gamal Hemdan
(geographer, "The Personality of Egypt"), and the
writing model Dr. Mohamed Ghoniem (kidney-transplant
pioneer) with three biography tips (say who and why
you chose them; what they did, in past simple; the
qualities that make them known) — plus a listening
dialog on Ahmed El-Mansy and an interview script with
Dr. Farouq El-Baz in U5's audioscripts. U4 a
decision-making story (Youssef's party) and an athlete
interview; writing = a blog post about a problem you
faced, with a BLOG TIP (a title that gets people
interested; details; images; end with a call to
action). U5 a careers advertisement and Omar's career
plan; writing = a REVIEW of a dream job, with its Tip
(describe what you are writing about; the good and bad
sides; what you think of it). U6 a school-handbook text
on global citizenship and an oceans text written to
persuade (its own questions name the kind: "a message
that tries to convince you"); writing = a SOCIAL MEDIA
POST, with a five-point Tip (a catchy intro; clear
short sentences; emojis and symbols — not too many; be
positive and kind; check spelling and grammar).

BOOK SLIPS, as printed: the U1 audioscript (p.104)
prints "transpartation" for "transportation." The
frequency ladder's rarely = 10% against Primary 5 Term
1's 20% is recorded above as a cross-book disagreement,
each as printed. Print spec (112 pages) matches the
PDF; no ISBN or deposit number.

Filed: map + curriculum note only — this book restates
the Primary system at Prep depth; its new named things
(punctuation line, parts of speech set, root families,
spidergram, synonym/antonym, collocations, connectors)
are recorded here and in the curriculum note until

Shape: the unit numbering CONTINUES the year — Units

U9 Make It Yourself p.40 · Review 3 p.54 · U10 Helping
Others p.57 · U11 The Natural World p.71 · U12 I Will

pp.101–103 · Notes pp.104–111. The teacher's front

pp.11–12 — this term it is a REVIEW TEST OF TERM 1:
a role-model dialog, an athlete text, Term 1 vocabulary
and make/do choices, verb forms across the Term 1
tenses, rewrites with can't / usually / should /
going to / mustn't, and the 80–100-word biography
task). TERM 2 CHANGES THE UNIT SHAPE: L5 is now
"Let's Talk" (a dialog lesson, "how to expand your
vocabulary") and L6 is the writing lesson — Term 1's

Self-Reflection pages: each unit now ends at its

GRAMMAR SPINE — Term 2 finishes gathering the Primary
systems, one per unit:
- U7 MODALS, the fullest single statement in the run
  (Tip, p.17, "Using Modal Verbs for Necessity, Advice,
  Unnecessity & Prohibition"): must = a strong rule,
  important obligation, or something you feel necessary;
  have to = something necessary because of rules, or
  when there is no other choice; mustn't = something is
  not allowed; should = a good idea / helpful advice;
  don't/doesn't have to = something is not necessary.
  (Primary 5 Term 2's set + Primary 6 Term 1's box,
  completed and gathered, with the feel-necessary vs
  rules split between must and have to stated.)
- U8 POSSESSIVES, the pair stated (p.30–31): Possessive
  adjectives show who something belongs to and come
  BEFORE a noun (my book, your cat, his car, her shoes,
  our house, their teacher); possessive pronouns show
  the same belonging but STAND ALONE, no noun after
  them (mine, yours, his, hers, ours, theirs).
- U9 THE POSSESSIVE FORMS completed in three boxes
  (pp.43–44): 's — with people, animals, time, and
  sometimes places; s' — plural nouns ending in s;
  of — with things, abstract ideas, and long phrases.
  Plus MAKE vs DO as a collocation Tip (p.42: make a
  sandwich, make coffee, make a mistake, make friends;
  do a job, do work, do well, do exercises, do the
  ironing, do cleaning) and a SEQUENCE WORDS Tip
  (p.42: when you describe a process or steps, use
  sequence words to show the order of actions — First,
  Next, Then, After that, Finally).
- U10 THE FUTURE SIMPLE, fullest statement (Tips,
  p.61): form — subject + will + base verb; negative
  will not (won't); question Will + subject + base
  verb? Uses named for the first time: QUICK DECISIONS,
  PROMISES / OFFERS, PREDICTIONS; time expressions
  tomorrow, next week, soon, later, in an hour, in the
  future. (Primary 3 Term 2's will/won't box, completed.)
- U11 COMPARATIVES & SUPERLATIVES restated compactly
  (Tip, p.74): comparatives compare two things,
  superlatives show the highest degree; short adjectives
  add -er/-est; long adjectives use more/most;
  irregulars good → better → best, bad → worse → worst
  (far, present in both Primary 5 books, is dropped
  here — as printed).
- U12 QUANTIFIERS, the table completed (p.89):
  countable nouns are things we can count one by one
  and take a, an, many, some, any, a lot of; uncountable
  nouns can't be counted separately and take much,
  some, any, a lot of; a/an with singular countable;
  many with plural countable; much with uncountable;
  a lot of with both; some in positive sentences; any
  in questions and negatives. (Primary 5 Term 1's
  a/an/some/any box + the counting line, completed
  with many / much / a lot of.)

THE STORY: ONE story now runs the WHOLE TERM — "The
Golden Goose," the Grimm tale adapted, one chapter per
unit (U7 Ch.1 The Three Brothers · U8 Ch.2 Dummling's
Kindness · U9 Ch.3 The Goose's Magic · U10 Ch.4 The
Long Procession · U11 Ch.5 The Princess's Laughter ·
U12 Ch.6 Dummling's Reward). Filed by summary only: a
poor man's three sons go to cut wood; the proud eldest
and the clever, unkind middle son each refuse an old
man's plea for food and are punished (a slipped axe, a
broken axe); the youngest, Dummling, mocked but kind,
shares his bread and drink, and the old man rewards
him with a goose of pure gold hidden in a tree. At a
hotel, the owner's three children try to steal a
feather and stick fast to the goose one after another;
Dummling walks on unaware, and the procession grows —
a schoolmaster, a farmer, a soldier all stick when
they try to pull the others free. The parade makes the
king's never-laughing princess laugh for the first
time; the king keeps his promise of her hand but sets
further challenges, which Dummling completes wisely
(told in a line, not shown); he marries the princess,
becomes a wise and gentle prince, and the tale's
closing lesson is that true wealth lies not in gold
but in kindness, modesty, and love. Reviews 3 and 4
each close with "The Story" questions on the chapters
so far.

TEXTS AND WRITING, briefly: U7 the Pharaohs' Golden
Parade text (April 3, 2021 — 22 royal mummies moved
from the Tahrir museum to the National Museum of
Egyptian Civilization) and a Grand Egyptian Museum
text (480,000 m², more than 100,000 artifacts, the
world's largest archaeological museum for a single
civilization, real photo); writing = the ADVICE BLOG,
defined by a Tip (a short online text where a writer
gives helpful tips, rules, or suggestions; writers use
modals like must, mustn't, should, have to, and don't
have to to give clear advice) with its must-include
list (a clear title, a short introduction, advice using
modals, correct punctuation and spelling, 80–100
words). U8 a Hassan-family roles text, a Garcia-family
traditions email (San Antonio, Texas), and the
DESCRIPTIVE PARAGRAPH with a "Tip for Describing
someone" (basic information — name, age, how you know
them; physical appearance — height, build, hair, eyes;
personality; interests; a conclusion that sums up) and
a five-part task frame. U9 a falafel-sandwich recipe
text, a cousin's-birthday preparation text, and
INSTRUCTIONS writing with the "How to Describe a
Process" Tip (1 sequence words to show order; 2 action
verbs; 3 the imperative form or the present simple;
4 short, clear sentences; 5 useful phrases — You need…,
Make sure you…, Don't forget to…, Be careful when…).
U10 a kindness story (Amal's visiting day for elderly
neighbors), three volunteer-interview listenings, and a
plans text with a six-point Writing Tip (plan your
ideas first; start sentences with I will… / We will…;
use the target vocabulary; give reasons; use linking
words; check spelling and grammar — will + infinitive).
U11 African natural wonders (Victoria Falls, Table
Mountain, Lake Malawi) and a nature-facts text
(giraffe, bamboo, the Amazon, the Venus flytrap); a
weather-report speaking frame (conditions, temperature
highs and lows, warnings, advice for listeners); and a
descriptive paragraph with the "Use Sensory Details"
Writing Tip (good writing helps the reader see, hear,
and feel the scene — what does it look like, sound
like, feel like). U12 a good-student text, Rania's
house-rules listening, a healthy-choices text (hygiene
defined inside it), a principal-and-students dialog
with a "How to Give Instructions / How to Respond" Tip
(start with a verb; polite forms; be clear and short),
and the RULES POSTER with a six-point poster Tip (a
clear title — short, big, eye-catching; short
sentences; bullet points or lists; imperatives; make
it attractive; check spelling and grammar).

(Creator Adobe InDesign 17.3, Producer Adobe PDF Library 16.0.7).

Identity: cover "ENGLISH — Preparatory Schools - Year Two -
Term 1 — 2025 - 2026 — Student's Book." Ministry message p.4 is
the same Preparatory-framework variant as Prep 7 (same Key
Features list). Teacher front section: Scope and Sequence
pp.5–7, Lesson Components explainer pages pp.8–10. Contents
