UNIT SHAPE (Prep 8 Term 1): six lessons per unit — L1 reading,
L2 listening + Language in Use, L3 reading, L4 Story Time,
L5 "Let's Talk" (a dialog lesson with a Conversation Tip and a
role-play), L6 writing (a "Before You Write" page: target
language, target vocabulary of at least 6 words, a model, the
task) — then Assessment Corner and a "My Self-Reflection" page
("I can…" by Reading / Vocabulary / Listening / Speaking /
Language / Writing / Life Skills & Values). Writing length steps
up: tasks are 90–100 words (Prep 7 asked 80–100). Think–Pair–
Share remains the standard speaking frame. Start Smart
(pp.11–12) is the diagnostic pre-test on the Prep 7 system.

GRAMMAR SPINE — the term is a gathering-and-extending pass over
the system at Prep 8 depth:
- U1: the present tenses together — present simple restated and
  present continuous with its uses box (actions happening right
  now; the arranged-future use is practised in the exercises).
- U2: modal verbs in use + the PASSIVE, boxed properly for the
  first time in the run: Present Simple Passive = object +
  am/is/are + past participle; Modal Passive = object + modal +
  be + past participle (p.31), taught through digital-footprint
  sentences (active ↔ passive transformations).
- U3: Past Simple and Past Continuous boxed side by side (p.45)
  — the interrupted-action pattern (while/when) practised as a
  pair; the past continuous Prep 7 T2 boxed alone is here set
  against the past simple in one place.
- U4: Connectors restated as one box (p.62): cause → because;
  result → so; contrast → but / although (Prep 7 T1's U6 set
  with but added to the contrast side).
- U5: Comparatives and Superlatives restated in use-based form
  (p.76 Tip).
- U6: the CONDITIONALS box (p.91) — First conditional (If +
  present simple, will + infinitive; real or possible future
  situations) AND the SECOND conditional, NEW in the run (If +
  past simple, would + infinitive; unreal or imaginary
  situations in present or future). The first conditional from
  Primary 6 T2 / Prep 7 T1 is now one half of a stated pair.

READING SKILLS named this term: identifying the main idea
(Reading Tips box, U1 L1: read fast, look for big clues, watch
the title, headings, and the first and last sentences of
paragraphs); INFERENCE defined + Fact vs Opinion (Reading Tips,
U5 L1, p.74 — telling a fact from an opinion is taught as a
reading move); Context Clues Tip (U6 L3, p.92: guess an unknown
word from the sentences before and after it); identifying the
speaker's tone, attitude or emotion (Listening Tip, U6 L2,
p.90).

REAL-TALK TIP (U5 L5, p.82) — a new named kind: in real
conversations we mix tenses; use the present perfect to talk
about experiences and the past simple for specific details or
history. (The book uses the present perfect in its dialogs and
self-reflection lines this term without boxing it here — it was
boxed in Prep 7 T2 U2.)

UNIT BY UNIT:
- U1 Gen Alpha (pp.13–26): L1 reading "Gen Alpha: The Young
  Digital Natives" (the generation born around 2010 to the
  mid-2020s, digital natives, screen time and balance) with the
  main-idea Reading Tips box and a vocabulary match defined
  in-lesson. L2 listening: the "Gen Alpha's Digital Life"
  podcast — an interview with Dr. Wessam (script p.103) — +
  present tenses. L3 reading "The Gen Z–Alpha Bridge" (Gen Z
  born 1997–2012, Gen Alpha from 2010; the digital language the
  two generations share). L4 Story Time: The Little Inventor
  Ch.1. L6 writing: a blog post about a daily routine using
  technology, written with the present tenses.
- U2 My Digital Footprint (pp.27–40): L1 reading on the digital
  footprint (online identity, data, traces, privacy settings).
  L2 listening: the "Web Guardians" podcast on staying safe
  online (script p.104: a digital footprint is "like a trail of
  breadcrumbs"; strong passwords, privacy settings, talk to a
  trusted adult) + the passive boxes. L3 "Managing Your Online
  Data." L4: The Little Inventor Ch.2. L6 writing: tips to
  protect your online privacy.
- U3 Facing Challenges (pp.41–54): L1 a motivational article on
  overcoming challenges (determination, obstacles, courage).
  L2 listening: Laila's race story (script pp.104–105 — she
  falls, her knee bleeds, she refuses to quit: summary only) +
  the past-tense pair. L3 "Facing Traffic Challenges": Cairo's
  traffic problems and Egypt's solutions (metro Lines 3 and 4,
  Tahya Misr Bridge, the electric train and the monorail).
  L4: The Little Inventor Ch.3 (the story ends). L6 writing: a
  paragraph (90–100 words) about a challenge you faced and
  overcame.
- Review 1 (pp.55–57): U1–U3 vocabulary and language; a Gen
  Alpha listening text (script p.105).
- U4 Art and Expression (pp.58–71): L1 an article about art and
  artists. L2 listening: an interview with the artist Youssef
  (script pp.105–106 — imagination, clay and metal sculpture,
  colourful paints) + the Connectors box. L3 an article about
  Mahmoud Mokhtar (born 1891, died 1934; the Nahdat Misr statue,
  placed in a Cairo square in 1928; art schools in Egypt and
  Paris) — a real artist, told as biography. L4: The Hidden
  Island Ch.1. L6 writing: an email about a kind of art you
  like.
- U5 Around the World (pp.72–86): L1 Rokaya's travel blog
  (Japan and Morocco) with the Inference box and the Fact vs
  Opinion Reading Tips (p.74). L2 listening: "A Tour Around
  Cairo" (script p.106 — the Pyramids of Giza, the Great
  Sphinx, the Grand Egyptian Museum, the New Administrative
  Capital, Khan El-Khalili Bazaar). L3 "Four Amazing Places"
  with REAL PHOTOGRAPHS: Petra, Machu Picchu, Reykjavik,
  Chefchaouen. L4: The Hidden Island Ch.2. L5 Let's Talk: the
  Nada-and-Noura dialog (the Colosseum, Big Ben, souvenirs)
  with a Conversation Tip (use comparatives and superlatives to
  compare places) and the Real Talk Tip on mixing tenses
  (p.82). L6 writing: a social media post (90–100 words) about
  a country you would like to visit; the Social Media Post Tip
  (p.84) restates Prep 7 T1's five points in full (catchy
  intro; clear short sentences; emojis and symbols in measure;
  be positive and kind; check spelling and grammar).
- U6 Young Innovators (pp.87–99): L1 reading "Making Your
  Ideas Happen" (find a problem, plan, build a model, test it,
  show or sell the real version; impact defined in-text as how
  an invention helps people). L2 listening: the biography of
  Ahmed Zewail (script pp.106–107: born in Damanhur in 1946,
  grew up in Alexandria, studied at Alexandria University,
  professor of chemistry at the California Institute of
  Technology, the femtosecond discovery, Nobel Prize in
  Chemistry 1999 — the first Egyptian and Arab scientist to
  receive the Nobel Prize in this field) with the tone
  Listening Tip and a REAL PHOTOGRAPH of Zewail (p.90). L3 "A
  Man of Impact": Hany Azer, the Egyptian engineer who was
  chief engineer for Berlin Main Station, with a REAL
  PHOTOGRAPH (p.92) and the Context Clues Tip. L4: The Hidden
  Island Ch.3 (the story ends). L5 Let's Talk: the Zeina-and-
  Lina dialog (brainstorming, feedback, a water-saving device)
  with a Conversation Tip on "if" sentences. L6 writing: a
  biography (90–100 words) about your role model; the model is
  a cloze biography of Ahmed Idris, the Nubian soldier whose
  unwritten-language idea gave the Egyptian army a secret code
  in the 1973 October War and who was honoured in 2017 with
  the Order of the Sinai Star — with a REAL PHOTOGRAPH of the
  honouring ceremony (p.97); the Biography Tip defines the
  kind: the story of a person's life — who they are, what they
  did, and why they are important.
- Review 2 (pp.100–102): Dina the sculptor listening (script
  p.107 — her statue of a bird became a symbol of hope); a
  family trip to see Mount Fuji reading; a paragraph (90–100
  words) on "The Importance of Innovation in Modern Life";
  sentence rewrites with the term's connectors and
  conditionals; an email (90–100 words) about a visit to a
  museum or art exhibition; a closing "The Story" section on
  The Hidden Island.

STORIES (summary only, as always): TWO chaptered stories, one
per half-term, three chapters each, one chapter in every unit's
Story Time lesson. (1) "The Little Inventor" (U1 Ch.1 "The
Creative Fair" p.20 · U2 Ch.2 "Never Stop Trying" p.34 · U3
Ch.3 "Inspiring Success" p.48): Amal, a curious village girl
the other children call strange, builds a talking robot for
her school's Creative Fair. She researches safely — she asks
permission before downloading anything, never shares her
personal information, checks that her sources are trustworthy,
and never copies others' work. The robot fails again and
again; her friend Yara encourages her with empathy and she
perseveres. At the fair the robot speaks its message about
being yourself and never giving up; Amal's pride is not in a
prize but in her honesty, determination, creativity, and
responsible use of digital tools. (2) "The Adventure of the
Hidden Island" (U4 Ch.1 "Different, but connected" p.65 · U5
Ch.2 "Freedom and Friendship" p.79 · U6 Ch.3 "Teamwork" p.93):
four friends — Hany, Faris, Malik, and Sami — find an old map
to a Hidden Island and sail to it under one rule, that no one
is the boss. On the island they explore in pairs, stay close
and safe, draw maps, and share stories; at sunset they sail
home, become known in their village as "The Island Explorers,"
and teach the other children to explore, create, and lead with
kindness, respect, and teamwork — leadership defined in-story
as caring for others and helping them shine.

BACK PAGE (p.112): developed and produced with the Content
Department Team at GPS Publishing and Printing Company (the
Prep run's publisher, as in Prep 7). Reviewed by the same
seven as Prep 7: Prof. Zeinab El-Naggar, Prof. Attia El-Sayed,
Dr. Mai Kamal, Mr. Reda Shalaby (spelled "Shalaby" here), Mr.
Mohamed Gomaa, Mr. Mostafa Zaghlol, Mr. Ayman Elsayed. CACD
team: Ms. Eman Mahmoud Youssef, Dr. Amira Fawzy Ahmed, Mr.
Mohammed Ibrahim Osman, Dr. Shaimaa Salah El Din Afify.
General supervision Dr. Akram Hassan Mohammed. British Council
+ CACD + GPS marks. Print spec: 112 pages (MATCHES the PDF),
27 × 19 cm, interior 70 g white paper, cover 180 g coated,
4 colours. NO ISBN, NO deposit number. Audioscripts
pp.103–107; Notes pp.108–111 are blank ruled pages.

Filing note: map + curriculum note only — the book gathers and
extends the Prep system (its one new grammar thing, the second
conditional, joins the conditionals when that lesson is built);
no built lesson's sources change.

## Egypt — Preparatory 8 Term 2 (English, Student's Book)

Source: downloaded direct from the Egypt MoE e-learning host,
2026/Prepratory/Prepratory2/Term2/StudentBook/English_prep2_Tr2.pdf
— the lowercase "prep2" stem is the live one (ten other stem and

egypt-grade-08-English_prep2_Tr2.pdf, is what gave the stem
away). 204,271,884 bytes; Last-Modified 17 Jan 2026 — the
ministry's raw file (Creator Adobe InDesign 17.3, Producer
Adobe PDF Library 16.0.7), taken per the edition rule.
112 pages. This book has NO text layer (pdftotext yields
nothing); it was read visually, all 112 pages. Printed folio =

Identity: cover "ENGLISH — Preparatory Schools - Year Two -
Term 2 — 2025 - 2026 — Student's Book." Ministry message p.3 =
the same Preparatory-framework variant. Front-matter order
differs from Term 1: Scope and Sequence pp.4–6, Lesson
Components pp.7–9, Contents p.10, Start Smart pp.11–12. UNIT
NUMBERING CONTINUES THE YEAR: U7 My School Life p.13 · U8
Learn Smart, Learn Easy p.26 · U9 Jobs & Skills p.39 · Review
3 p.53 · U10 Storytelling p.56 · U11 Life in the Desert p.69 ·
U12 Our Incredible Earth p.84 · Review 4 p.100 · Audioscripts
pp.103–105 · Notes pp.106–111.

UNIT SHAPE: the Term 2 shape — L1 reading, L2 listening +
Language in Use, L3 reading, L4 Story Time, L5 "Let's Talk"
(dialog + Conversation Tip + role-play), L6 writing; units end
at the Assessment Corner (no Self-Reflection page in Term 2).
Writing tasks stay 90–100 words. A PEER CHECK step appears in
the writing lessons (partners exchange work and check against
a printed list). Start Smart (pp.11–12) reviews Term 1: a
generations dialog, a technology-safety reading, and a
90–100-word social media post about a tourist place visited.

GRAMMAR SPINE — one new tense, the rest gathering and
deepening:
- U7: IMPERATIVES boxed (Tip p.17): imperatives give orders,
  instructions, or advice; the base form of the verb without a
  subject; the negative is "Don't + infinitive." With it,
  Should & Shouldn't for giving advice (should = a good idea,
  shouldn't = a bad idea) restated as a pair.
- U8: CONTRAST CONNECTORS in full form-and-usage form (Tip
  p.30): BUT joins two clauses in one sentence (direct
  contrast); HOWEVER starts a new sentence, is more formal
  than "but," and is always followed by a comma; WHILE shows
  contrast between two simultaneous ideas and can start a
  sentence or come in the middle. The connectors line deepens:
  cause/result/contrast (Prep 7 T1) → but added to the
  contrast side (Prep 8 T1) → this form-and-usage treatment.
- U9: CAUSE & EFFECT (Tip p.43): because / since show the
  reason; so / therefore / as a result show the result.
- U10: the PAST PERFECT, NEW in the run (Tip p.60): form had +
  past participle; used for an action that happened before
  another past action or time; its common connectors are
  before, after, because, and by the time. Taught inside the
  storytelling unit, for sequencing story events.
- U11: Past Simple and Past Continuous restated WITH FORMS
  (Tip p.73): past simple = subject + verb(-ed) / irregular
  past form, for finished actions and sequences of events;
  past continuous = subject + was/were + verb(-ing), for
  actions in progress and background situations; Combined Use:
  the past continuous is the background, the past simple is
  the interrupting action. The fullest statement of the pair
  in the run.
- U12: the PRESENT PERFECT restated with form and three uses
  (Tip p.89): have/has + past participle — experiences in life
  (without saying exactly when); actions that started in the
  past and continue until now; recently completed actions
  (often with "just," "already," or "yet" — the "yet" sits in
  this third line as printed).

STORY-KIND DEFINITIONS — this term names the kinds themselves:
a FOLKTALE is a traditional story passed from one generation
to another, usually teaching a moral or lesson through simple
characters like farmers, animals, or children (Tip, U10 L2,
p.59); a FAIRY TALE is a short story that includes magic,
imaginary characters such as fairies, giants, or talking
animals, often begins with "Once upon a time" and ends with
"Happily ever after," and usually teaches a moral or lesson
(Tip, U10 L3, p.61). Story elements are named on the U10
opener: Beginning, Problem, Climax, Ending.

UNIT BY UNIT:
- U7 My School Life (pp.13–25): L1 "School Rules" reading
  (dress code, behavior rules, library rules — must/should in
  the text itself). L2 "My School Day" listening (a spoken set
  of school-day advice; script p.103) + the imperatives Tip.
  L3 "Staying Organized with a School Timetable" article. L4:
  The Jungle Book Ch.1. L5 Let's Talk: Ahmed and Mahmoud plan
  changes to the school rules and timetable (Conversation Tip:
  phrases for giving advice, agreeing politely, making a
  plan). L6 writing: a SCHOOL REPORT (90–100 words) — the
  report kind, with a Writing Tip (title; introduction;
  paragraphs, each about one idea; time words; a conclusion
  with your opinion or feeling).
- U8 Learn Smart, Learn Easy (pp.26–38): L1 "Can Games Make
  You Smarter?" reading. L2 listening: an interview with the
  teacher Mrs. Manal on making learning fun (script
  pp.103–104) + the contrast-connectors Tip. L3 "Singing,
  Stories, and Smart Learning" reading. L4: The Jungle Book
  Ch.2. L5 Let's Talk: Samir and Maher trade study methods,
  plus a group debate (Books vs Games vs Videos vs Comics)
  with sample arguments printed for each team. L6 writing: a
  STUDY TIPS POSTER with a Poster Structure Tip (Title; three
  tips, each [Advice] + [Reason]; "Remember:" — a final
  encouraging message).
- U9 Jobs & Skills (pp.39–52): L1 "Job Profiles" — a JOB
  PROFILE defined in the text (a short but clear description
  of a person's work, skills, and responsibilities; a guide
  that helps employers and employees understand what is
  expected in a role) with a Job Profile Tip (use the present
  tense to describe what the person does). L2 "Workers'
  Stories" listening: four workers speak (Ali the bus driver,
  Samir the mechanic, Hassan the chef, Rana the journalist;
  script p.104) + the cause & effect Tip; a synonym/antonym
  table is worked from a dictionary in this lesson. L3
  "Career Advice" reading. L4: The Jungle Book Ch.3. L5 Let's
  Talk: Sara and Lina on interests, values, and job choice.
  L6 writing: a paragraph (90–100 words) about a future job,
  with a Useful Language Phrases Tip (describing interests;
  explaining reasons; future expressions; giving examples)
  and a Peer Check list.
- Review 3 (pp.53–55): U7–U9 — a games listening (script
  p.104), a healthy-habits paragraph, a Study Tips poster
  task, and "The Story" questions on The Jungle Book.
- U10 Storytelling (pp.56–68): L1 "Salma's Treasure," a short
  story (summary in the story note). L2 "Folktales from
  Around the World" listening (the farmer-and-the-bird
  folktale; script p.104) with the Folktale Tip + the past
  perfect Tip; speaking = retelling a story in five sentences
  with the time connectors first, then, after that, finally.
  L3 "Story Structures": Cinderella retold (summary in the
  story note) with the Fairy Tale Tip. L4: The Jungle Book
  Ch.4. L5 Let's Talk: Salma and Yomna choose stories to tell
  the class ("The Lion and the Mouse"; "The Seven Voyages of
  Sinbad the Sailor" — named, not retold). L6 writing:
  WRITING A STORY ENDING, with a model story read by its
  sections (beginning / middle / ending) and a Useful
  Language Tip (describing feelings; showing cause & effect;
  adding twists; wrapping up). Assessment writing: a story of
  90–100 words.
- U11 Life in the Desert (pp.69–83): L1 "Desert Communities"
  (the Bedouin nomadic lifestyle; the Hopi people's
  irrigation; modern desert towns conserving water and using
  solar energy). L2 "Desert Survival Stories" listening —
  three first-person accounts (Sarah, lost in the Sahara for
  two days; Ahmed the desert guide, whose jeep broke down 50
  miles from town; Mariam the researcher, caught by a
  sandstorm; scripts pp.104–105) + the past-tense pair Tip.
  L3 "Desert Wildlife" (adaptations defined in the matching:
  changes that help living things survive; the fennec fox,
  camels storing fat in their humps, cactus and aloe storing
  water — note the reading says camels store FAT in their
  humps; the lesson's own true/false still tests "Camels
  store water in their humps" as a statement to judge). L4:
  The Jungle Book Ch.5. L5 Let's Talk: Nora and Soha compare
  desert life and city life beside a printed compare chart,
  then a class debate ("Desert life is better than city
  life"). L6 writing: A DESERT DIARY entry (90–100 words) —
  the diary kind, with a Diary Entry Structure Tip (Greeting:
  Dear Diary; Opening; Morning / Daytime / Evening sections;
  Feelings; Closing with future plans) and sensory detail
  (what you saw, heard, felt, smelled) required.
- U12 Our Incredible Earth (pp.84–99): L1 "Earth Facts"
  (Earth is about 4.5 billion years old; the Sahara is so huge
  astronauts can see it from space; the Amazon rainforest is
  "the lungs of the Earth"; Mount Everest; gravity holds the
  atmosphere in place; humans have explored only about 5% of
  the oceans). L2 "Natural Wonders" listening interview (the
  Grand Canyon, the Amazon, deep oceans, Son Doong Cave,
  bioluminescent beaches in the Maldives, Iceland's
  volcanoes, Antarctica's icebergs; script p.105) + the
  present perfect Tip. L3 "Environment Reports" reading
  (deforestation, endangered species, recycling, solar
  panels). L4: The Jungle Book Ch.6 — the story ends. L5
  Let's Talk: a reporter interviews an environmental expert,
  with a Conversation Tip for organising environmental talk
  (state the problem; suggest solutions; explain the reason;
  end with a positive message). L6 writing: a "SAVE THE
  EARTH" POSTER, with a Writing-a-Poster Tip (a short, clear,
  catchy title; short sentences and action words; colours,
  pictures, and clear spacing; a slogan or call to action).
- Review 4 (pp.100–102): U10–U12 — an environment listening
  (script p.105), a desert reading, "The Story" questions on
  The Jungle Book's ending, and a "Saving Energy" paragraph
  (90–100 words).

STORY (summary only): ONE story across the whole term — "The
Jungle Book" by Rudyard Kipling, adapted, six chapters, one in
every unit's Story Time lesson, with an author introduction in
Ch.1 (Kipling 1865–1936, born in India; The Jungle Book 1894;
the first English writer to win the Nobel Prize in Literature,
1907). Ch.1 "Founding in the Jungle" (U7, p.20): a baby boy
wanders from his village and is found by Father Wolf and
Mother Wolf; Shere Khan the tiger demands the child; Mother
Wolf defies him; Baloo the bear and Bagheera the panther
support her, Bagheera offering a freshly killed ox; the
child, named Mowgli ("little frog"), joins the Seeonee wolf
pack, and Shere Khan swears he will destroy the man-cub one
day. Ch.2 "The Law of the Jungle" (U8, p.33): Baloo trains
Mowgli in the Law of the Jungle and Bagheera teaches hiding,
climbing, and self-defense; the Bandar-log monkeys kidnap
Mowgli with false promises; Baloo and Bagheera enlist Kaa the
python, and together they rescue him — Mowgli learns that not
all who call themselves friends can be trusted, and that true
friendship is loyalty and respect. Ch.3 "The Secret of Fire"
(U9, p.46): Shere Khan whispers lies to the pack; Bagheera
warns Mowgli that men have one power the jungle fears —
fire; Mowgli steals a pot of burning coals from the village
and, at the wolf council, reveals the fire and drives Shere
Khan back — but he is sad, because many wolves no longer see
him as one of their own. Ch.4 "Mowgli's Final Confrontation"
(U10, p.63): Shere Khan sets a trap, driving cattle toward
Mowgli; brave and resourceful, Mowgli turns the plan against
the tiger with fire and cunning, trapping him in a narrow
pass, where the tiger is crushed and finally defeated;
Mowgli feels both victory and sadness, decides to leave the
pack, and the chapter marks the end of his childhood. Ch.5
"Mowgli in the Human Village" (U11, p.77): Mowgli enters the
human village; the people feed and house him, but many doubt
him and whisper that he is dangerous or cursed; he protects
the village's cattle from predators and learns more about
fire and tools, yet longs for the open forest and the honesty
of the Law — caught between doubt from men and rejection from
wolves, he feels isolated, and his greatest challenge is no
longer Shere Khan. Ch.6 "Mowgli's Life Balance" (U12, p.93):
over time Mowgli accepts that he belongs to both worlds; he
returns often to the jungle, where Baloo, Bagheera, and his
wolf brothers welcome him as family, and he defends the
jungle against hunters and the villagers against wild animals
— a bridge between man and nature; the book's own closing
says the story ends not with Mowgli choosing one world over
the other, but with him welcoming both.
Also in U10: "Salma's Treasure" (L1) — Salma opens an old
wooden box and finds a faded letter her grandfather wrote to
her mother decades earlier, wishing her a life of courage,
kindness, and strong hope; the letter itself is the treasure,
kept for generations. The L2 folktale (script p.104): a kind,
poor farmer frees a small bird trapped in a net; the bird
becomes a young man who stays and works the farm in
gratitude, asking only that the farmer never ask about his
past; the farmer promises — but one day curiosity wins, he
asks, and the young man sadly leaves forever; the lesson the
farmer learns is to keep his promise. Cinderella (L3) is
retold in short form: the kind girl, the cruel stepmother and
two unkind stepsisters, the kind fairy's gifts (a dazzling
dress, glass slippers, a pumpkin turned golden carriage,
mice turned horses), the royal party, the midnight flight and
the lost slipper, the prince's search, the marriage — her
kindness finally rewarded with love and happiness.

phrases, fluency vs accuracy, repeating key words to show
you are listening). Start Smart (pp.11–12) reviews the Prep 8
year: a weekend dialog, a Sahara Desert reading, and a
90-word paragraph, "Your school."

GRAMMAR SPINE — the passive and the conditionals are
completed, and reported speech opens:
- U1: the PRESENT PERFECT, ACTIVE AND PASSIVE together (Tips
  p.17). Active: have / has + the past participle — the three
  uses restated (actions or experiences that happened at an
  unspecified time in the past; actions that started in the
  past and continue till now; recently completed actions).
  Passive (NEW): have / has + been + the past participle —
  "The homework has been done"; "The Pyramids have been
  visited." With it, a SINCE / FOR Tip: since + point of time
  ("I've spoken English since 1992"); for + period of time
  ("I've spoken English for 20 years").
- U2: the THIRD CONDITIONAL (Tip p.31): used to talk about
  unreal situations in the past. Form: If + past perfect
  (had + past participle), would have + past participle. It
  shows an imaginary situation and a different result that
  could have happened — but didn't; used to express regret,
  missed chances, and excuses. The conditionals now stand as
  a stated set of three — first (Prep 7), second (Prep 8),
  third (here); a "zero conditional" is never named anywhere
  in the run.
- U3: the FUTURE SIMPLE PASSIVE (Tip p.45): used to describe
  actions that will be done in the future — will be + past
  participle, shown against its active pair ("We will finish
  this project next Friday" → "This project will be finished
  next Friday"). The passive line now runs: present simple
  passive + modal passive (Prep 8 T1) → present perfect
  passive (U1 here) → future simple passive (here).
- U4: modals gathered by USE (Tip (1) p.63): must for
  obligation; can / can't for permission; mustn't for
  prohibition; should / shouldn't for advice. And a
  Connectors Tip (2): While / Although / Even though for
  contrast, and "On one hand / On the other hand" for BALANCE
  (a new connector job).
- U5: SEQUENCE ADVERBS as a set (Tip (1) p.75): First /
  Firstly → to begin with; Next, Then, After that → following
  this; Finally / Lastly, In conclusion → to end. And
  Imperatives restated as "What are imperatives?" (Tip (2)
  p.76): positive imperatives tell someone to do something;
  negative imperatives tell someone not to do something;
  "Never" instead of "Don't" is a powerful form of
  prohibition.
- U6: REPORTED SPEECH — reported statements (Tip p.89): when
  we report what someone said without quoting their exact
  words, we use reported speech; pronouns change depending on
  who is speaking and to whom; we change present sentences
  into past ("I play tennis" → He said that he played
  tennis); time expressions change — today → that day,
  yesterday → the day before / the previous day, tomorrow →
  the following day / the day after, now → then, here →
  there. Only statements are treated; reported questions and
  orders do not appear in this book.

UNIT BY UNIT:
- U1 Personal Identity (pp.13–26): L1 "Beyond My Looks,"
  reading "Who I Am Beyond My Looks" (Ahmed, 15, from Giza:
  identity is not about what others see; values, beliefs,
  self-discovery, strength, self-respect). L2 "Self-Discovery"
  listening: an interview with Leila, a 15-year-old student
  from Alexandria (script p.103), plus a second listening
  text about DR. MAGDI YACOUB (script pp.103–104 — real
  person; see the people note). + the present perfect Tips.
  L3 "The Mirror Moment" (Nour, who hides in the background
  and draws real faces, presents her Identity Map board to
  the class; her classmate Leila gives her a thumbs-up; Ms.
  Hanan tells her being different is her strength). L4: The
  School Garden Project Ch.1. L5 Let's Talk: Hala and Omar on
  respecting personal identity (Conversation Tip: positive
  body language — eye contact and nodding; Real Talk Tip:
  friendly phrases). L6 writing: a DESCRIPTIVE PARAGRAPH
  (110 words), the model annotated by its parts — Topic
  Sentence / Explanation / Evidence–Example / Ending
  Sentence — with a Tip defining the kind by those parts.
- U2 Communication with Family and Friends (pp.27–40): L1
  "Stay Connected" reading (communication defined by its
  reach: words, tone of voice, facial expressions, body
  language; misunderstanding; resolving conflict through
  calm, honest discussion). L2 listening: Mariam on
  communication challenges and solutions (script p.104) + the
  third conditional Tip. L3 "The Silent Dinner" (Omar's
  family has stopped talking at dinner; one honest sentence
  from Omar breaks the silence — summary in the story note).
  L4: The School Garden Project Ch.2. L5 Let's Talk: Mona and
  Naglaa repair a misunderstanding (the third conditional
  used live in the dialog) + a Real Talk Tip matching chatty
  phrases to their meanings ("That's cool!" = that's nice;
  "For sure" = no doubt). L6 writing: an OPINION paragraph
  (110 words) built as a "Learning-from-Experience
  Paragraph," with Helpful Hints (introduction — the opening
  sentence giving your opinion; briefly explain what
  happened; personal feelings and thoughts; the lesson
  learned; a closing sentence showing why the experience is
  important).
- U3 Artificial Intelligence (pp.41–54): L1 reading defines
  the AI / robot difference in the text: a robot is a machine
  that can move and do tasks; AI is the "brain" that can go
  inside a robot, letting it make decisions, learn from
  actions, and change its behavior — and not all AI is in
  robots, and not all robots are smart. L2 "AI Technology"
  listening (Adham and Lina on AI making art and music;
  script pp.104–105) + the future simple passive Tip. L3 "A
  Robot Teacher," fiction (summary in the story note). L4:
  The School Garden Project Ch.3. L5 Let's Talk: Eman and Ali
  on AI's future (Conversation Tip: ask open-ended questions
  to keep a conversation going). L6 writing: an EMAIL (about
  110 words) about the benefits of AI in the future, the
  model set out as an email (To / From / Subject) with
  Helpful Hints (start with "Hi [friend's name]"; say why you
  are writing; short and simple sentences; finish with a
  closing sentence or phrase like "Write back soon!" or
  "Talk to you later!").
- Review 1 (pp.55–58): U1–U3 — a dialog, a listening text
  "Egypt – Where the Past Meets the Future" (the New
  Administrative Capital's smart technology; solar power;
  script p.105), an education-and-AI reading, and "The Story"
  questions on The School Garden Project.
- U4 Screen Time (pp.59–71): L1 reading defines SCREEN TIME
  in the text (the total time a person spends looking at
  screens) and gives the experts' guidance (young people
  should not spend more than two hours a day on entertainment
  screen time). L2 listening: "Smart Habits for Egypt's Smart
  Future" (the New Administrative Capital, the Monorail, the
  Green River; script pp.105–106) + the two Tips (modals by
  use; connectors for contrast and balance). L3 "Balancing
  Screen Time," a first-person diary-style reading (a writer
  shocked by a six-hour daily phone report makes a plan). L4:
  The School Garden Project Ch.4. L5 Let's Talk: a mother and
  son negotiate screen-time balance. L6 writing: a BLOG POST
  (about 110 words), "How can you improve your screen time
  habits?", with Hints (start with a title that gets people
  interested; give details about the topic; use images
  related to the topic; end with a call to action).
- U5 Design Thinking (pp.72–84): L1 "Think Like a Designer"
  reading: DESIGN THINKING defined in the text (caring about
  people and imagining better solutions; the designer's three
  questions — "Who will use it?" "What do they need?" "How
  can I make it better?"), and a PROTOTYPE defined (simple
  models designers test). L2 listening: Sara's blog about a
  DESIGN SPRINT (script p.106 — a design sprint is when a
  team solves a big problem in just five days; her team:
  define the problem, interview 20 students, brainstorm over
  50 ideas, build prototypes, test with real students) + the
  sequence adverbs and imperatives Tips. L3 "See through
  Their Eyes" (Omar's class designs for new students; he
  spends a day with Karim, a new student, before
  brainstorming — the lesson: good design starts with real
  people and real feelings). L4: The School Garden Project
  Ch.5. L5 Let's Talk: Amir and Magdy walk the design-thinking
  steps in dialog — understand the users and what they need;
  think of many ideas; make a prototype; test it and ask
  people what they think; fix it and make it better. L6
  writing: a REVIEW (about 110 words) of a group's design
  project, with a "Writing about a problem-solving process"
  Tip (start with the problem; state your idea or solution;
  explain how you will test the idea; tell the feedback;
  describe how you will improve it).
- U6 Why do We Like Stories? (pp.85–99): L1 "The Power of
  Stories" reading (stories shape our thinking; we enter the
  minds of different characters; stories introduce different
  points of view, cultures, and lifestyles, and develop
  empathy). L2 listening: Ali tells Omar how a story he read
  — about a man who stayed respectful even when people were
  unfair — taught him to stay calm and respectful in a class
  debate (script pp.106–107) + the reported speech Tip. L3
  "Elements of a Story": "The Clever Fox" fable retold
  (summary in the story note), with Tip (1) TYPES OF STORIES
  — a seven-kind list, each defined: Fiction (stories made up
  with imaginary characters); Non-fiction (true stories based
  on real events and characters); Folk stories (traditional
  stories with lessons); Adventure stories (exciting journeys
  by brave characters); Comic stories (funny stories full of
  humor); Historical stories (based on real events from the
  past); Fables (short tales that teach a moral, often with
  talking animals) — and Tip (2) STORY ELEMENTS: Setting
  (where and when the story happens); Characters (people or
  animals in a story); Conflict (the problem in the story);
  Solution (how the problem ends); plus a story-map graphic
  organiser (Beginning / Middle / End — Who? Where? What
  happened? How did it end?). L4: The School Garden Project
  Ch.6 — the story ends. L5 Let's Talk: Sarah and Amal discuss
  why stories are powerful, entirely in reported speech. L6
  writing: "My Own Story" (about 110 words), the cloze model
  being the fable "The Dog and his Shadow," with a
  Writing-a-Story Tip (choose an interesting title; plan your
  story — Beginning: introduce the setting and characters;
  Middle: show the problem or exciting events; Ending: solve
  the problem and give a clear ending; use clear and simple
  language; use sequence words — first, then, after that,
  finally to show order; add a lesson or message; check your
  writing).
- Review 2 (pp.100–102): U4–U6 — a screen-time listening
  (Sarah spends six hours daily on her phone; script p.107),
  a technology-and-teenagers reading, "The Story" questions,
  and the 100–110-word story task "The Day I Learned to
  Balance Technology and Life."

STORY (summary only): ONE story across the whole term — "The
School Garden Project," an original story written for this
book, six chapters, one in every unit's Story Time lesson.
Zeina, a new student at a preparatory school, and her friend
Nancy. Ch.1 "A New Challenge" (U1, p.20): Zeina finds the
school playground large but empty and gray — concrete,
benches, trash on the ground; she asks Nancy, "What if we
planted a garden?"; she admits honestly that she doesn't know
how to grow plants but wants to learn together; the garden
could be more than a place — a way to bring the school
together. Ch.2 "Taking Initiative and Building a Team" (U2,
pp.34–35): Zeina researches school gardens and writes a
simple proposal, explaining both the good and the difficult
sides; at the student council meeting her hands shake, and
some students laugh — "You're just a new student; why should
we listen to you?" — but she answers, "This is my school too";
Ms. Hoda, the biology teacher, backs her determination, and
a team forms around the students' talents: Nancy good with
tools, Noha loved painting, Amal strong, Sarah organized.
Ch.3 "Facing Problems with Determination" (U3, p.48): the
team needs money for seeds, tools, and soil; Zeina shows
initiative, asking the local community for donations and
always being honest about how the money would be used; then
it rains for two weeks and some first plants die; Amal wants
to quit; Zeina holds the team together kindly; they research
which plants grow best in their climate and build a better
watering system, and the garden starts to look better again.
Ch.4 "Growing Success" (U4, p.65): after three months the
garden looks amazing; students eat lunch among the flowers
and the cafeteria uses some of the garden vegetables; Zeina
learns that being a leader doesn't mean always having the
answers — it means being responsible for both the successes
and the mistakes, and when some plants didn't grow well she
didn't blame anyone; the team uses eco-friendly methods only
(natural fertilizers, reduced trash); students from other
classes visit and ask how they can help. Ch.5 "The Impact of
Growth" (U5, p.78): at the end of the school year the garden
is full of green plants, colors, and life — but the biggest
change was in the students; they throw less trash and respect
the environment; Nancy discovers a love for growing food and
wants to study agriculture; Noha's signs and drawings make
the garden beautiful; Amal, who had almost quit, becomes one
of the most dedicated gardeners; Zeina realizes that being
honest with others — and with yourself — helps people trust
you, and that talking about her limits made her a better
leader. Ch.6 "Looking Forward" (U6, p.92): the next year,
Zeina is chosen president of the student council; she has
learned that being a real leader doesn't mean doing
everything alone — it means helping others and being honest
when things are hard; her ambition is now bigger than just a
garden: she wants to help students believe they could change
their school, and maybe the world; the garden keeps growing,
with more plants, painted benches, and quiet spaces for
reading, and becomes a symbol of what students could do
together; growth doesn't happen only in plants — it also
happens in people, especially when they show courage to try
something new and strength when things go wrong; small ideas
can lead to big changes — when people believe in them.
Also: "The Mirror Moment" (U1 L3) — Nour, a quiet Grade 9
student with curls and glasses who feels she doesn't fit in,
loves drawing faces — not perfect ones, but real ones; Ms.
Hanan sets a project, "Create your Identity Map: show us who
you are — your dreams, values, and challenges"; Nour panics,
then draws herself truly and presents a board of hand-drawn
portraits with her own face at the center — "Strong.
Creative. Curious."; the class claps, Leila gives a
thumbs-up, and Ms. Hanan tells her she showed the class that
being different is her strength. "The Silent Dinner" (U2 L3)
— Omar's family has grown distant: school stress, phone
distractions, arguments over small things, silence at
dinner; one night Omar speaks — he feels they've all been
strangers lately; his mother agrees they've been too busy or
too tired to talk; his father says, "Let's fix that"; the
conversation is short, but from that night their dinners
slowly fill with laughter again — reconnecting took a small,
meaningful gesture, not shouting or long messages. "A Robot
Teacher" (U3 L3) — one rainy morning Sara finds a shiny robot
teacher in her classroom; for a week it customizes lessons,
gives Nader fun math games, and checks all homework
instantly; but Sara misses her real teacher — the robot is
helpful, yet it doesn't smile or notice when students feel
tired or sad; when the human teacher returns, everyone has
learned that technology can help, but it cannot replace the
care and connection of a real teacher. "The Clever Fox" (U6
L3) — the fox flatters the crow's looks and voice until she
opens her beak to sing, and the cheese falls; the fox
catches it and runs away. "The Dog and his Shadow" (U6 L6, a
cloze model) — a hungry dog carrying a bone sees his
reflection in a stream, takes it for another dog with a
bigger bone, barks greedily to frighten it, and loses his
own bone in the water.

PEOPLE NOTE: the U1 L2 listening text about Dr. Magdi Yacoub
(scripts pp.103–104) is biographical: born in Egypt in 1935;
as a young man he enjoyed reading about science and
medicine; when his father's sister died from heart disease,
he felt very sad and began to wonder how doctors could save
people with heart problems; the question stayed in his mind
for years; he studied medicine and learned everything about
the heart; by following his curiosity he became a heart
surgeon, and discovered his true purpose: to save lives.
Ahmed Zewail (Prep 7–8) and Magdi Yacoub are the run's two
real Egyptian scientists given full life-story treatment.
